Smith (who abides in all lodging houses) tucked into the frame of the mirror. “Will Mrs. Smith please call Stockton 6771,” it said. A carpeted stair with a fine old mahogany balustrade rose into the dimness. Aubrey, who was thoroughly familiar with lodgings, knew instinctively that the fourth, ninth, tenth, and fourteenth steps would be creakers. A soft musk sweetened the warm, torpid air: he divined that someone was toasting marshmallows over a gas jet. He knew perfectly well that somewhere in the house would be a placard over a bathtub with the legend: “Please leave this tub as you would wish to find it.” Roger Mifflin would have said, after studying the hall, that someone in the house was sure to be reading the poems of Rabbi Tagore; but Aubrey was not so caustic.

===Chinese===
奥布里走进大厅，随手关上门。一面巨大的镜子模糊地反射着煤气灯微弱的奶黄色闪烁。他注意到一幅兰西尔（Landseer）的版画挂在仿灰色大石块图案的壁纸上，以及通常给那位常驻所有出租屋的J. F. 史密斯太太（Mrs. J. F. Smith）的电话备忘录夹在镜框里。“请史密斯太太致电Stockton 6771，”上面写道。一段铺着地毯的楼梯，配有精美的老式桃花心木栏杆，向上延伸至昏暗中。奥布里对出租屋非常熟悉，本能地知道第四、第九、第十和第十四级台阶会吱嘎作响。一种柔和的麝香甜味弥漫在温暖而沉滞的空气中：他推测有人在煤气灯上烤棉花糖。他清楚地知道，这栋房子的某处肯定会有一块浴缸上的标语牌，上面写着：“请将浴缸保持成您希望发现的样子。”罗杰·米夫林（Roger Mifflin）研究大厅后可能会说，这栋房子里肯定有人在读泰戈尔（Tagore）拉比的诗；但奥布里没有这么尖刻。

===Original===
Mrs. Schiller came up the basement stairs, followed by a small pug dog. She was warm and stout, with a tendency to burst just under the armpits. She was friendly. The pug made merry over Aubrey’s ankles.

===Chinese===
施勒太太从地下室楼梯上来，身后跟着一只小巴哥犬。她热情而丰满，腋下附近有绷开的趋势。她很友善。巴哥犬对奥布里的脚踝嬉戏不已。

===Original===
“Stop it, Treasure!” said Mrs. Schiller.

===Chinese===
“住手，宝贝（Treasure）！”施勒太太说。

===Original===
“Can I get a room here?” asked Aubrey, with great politeness.

===Chinese===
“我能在这里租个房间吗？”奥布里非常礼貌地问。

===Original===
“Third floor front’s the only thing I’ve got,” she said. “You don’t smoke in bed, do you? The last young man I had burned holes in three of my sheets—”

===Chinese===
“只有三楼朝前的一间空房，”她说。“你不在床上抽烟吧？我上一个年轻房客在我三条床单上烧了洞——”

===Original===
Aubrey reassured her.

===Chinese===
奥布里让她放心。

===Original===
“I don’t give meals.”

===Chinese===
“我不提供餐食。”

===Original===
“That’s all right,” said Aubrey. “Suits me.”

===Chinese===
“没关系，”奥布里说。“正合我意。”

===Original===
“Five dollars a week,” she said.

===Chinese===
“每周五美元，”她说。

===Original===
“May I see it?”

===Chinese===
“我能看看吗？”

===Original===
Mrs. Schiller brightened the gas and led the way upstairs. Treasure skipped up the treads beside her. The sight of the six feet ascending together amused Aubrey. The fourth, ninth, tenth, and fourteenth steps creaked, as he had guessed they would. On the landing of the second storey a transom gushed orange light. Mrs. Schiller was secretly pleased at not having to augment the gas on that landing. Under the transom and behind a door Aubrey could hear someone having a bath, with a great sloshing of water. He wondered irreverently whether it was Mrs. J. F. Smith. At any rate (he felt sure), it was some experienced habitué of lodgings, who knew that about five-thirty in the afternoon is the best time for a bath—before cooking supper and the homecoming ablutions of other tenants have exhausted the hot water boiler.

===Chinese===
施勒太太点亮煤气灯，带路上楼。宝贝在她身边轻快地跳上台阶。看到这两双六英尺的腿一起上楼，奥布里觉得很有趣。第四、第九、第十和第十四级台阶吱嘎作响，正如他所料。二楼楼梯平台上，一扇气窗涌出橙色的灯光。施勒太太暗自高兴，不用在那层楼梯平台上增加煤气灯了。在气窗下、一扇门后，奥布里能听到有人在洗澡，水花四溅。他不敬地想，那会不会是J. F. 史密斯太太。无论如何（他确信），是某个经验丰富的出租屋常客，知道下午五点半左右是洗澡的最佳时间——在做饭和其它租户回家洗漱用光热水器之前。

===Original===
They climbed one more flight. The room was small, occupying half the third-floor frontage. A large window opened onto the street, giving a plain view of the bookshop and the other houses across the way. A washstand stood modestly inside a large cupboard. Over the mantel was the familiar picture—usually, however, reserved for the fourth floor back—of a young lady having her shoes shined by a ribald small boy.

===Chinese===
他们又爬了一层楼。房间很小，只占三楼前部的一半。一扇大窗户朝向街道，可以清楚地看到书店和街对面的其他房子。一个洗脸台谦逊地立在一个大橱柜里。壁炉架上方是那幅熟悉的画——通常，不过，是留给四楼后面的房间的——画中一位年轻女士正让一个顽皮的小男孩擦鞋。

===Original===
Aubrey was delighted. “This is fine,” he said. “Here’s a week in advance.”

===Chinese===
奥布里很高兴。“这很好，”他说。“这是一周的预付租金。”

===Original===
Mrs. Schiller was almost disconcerted by the rapidity of the transaction. She preferred to solemnize the reception of a new lodger by a little more talk—remarks about the weather, the difficulty of getting “help,” the young women guests who empty tea-leaves down washbasin pipes, and so on. All this sort of gossip, apparently aimless, has a very real purpose: it enables the defenceless landlady to size up the stranger who comes to prey upon her. She had hardly had a good look at this gentleman, nor even knew his name, and here he had paid a week’s rent and was already installed.

===Chinese===
施勒太太几乎被交易的迅速搞得不知所措。她更喜欢用多一点谈话来庄严地迎接新房客——关于天气、请“帮手”的困难、把茶叶渣倒进洗脸盆管道的年轻女房客等等。所有这些看似漫无目的的闲聊，实际上都有一个非常真实的目的：它能让毫无防备的房东太太看清前来占她便宜的陌生人。她几乎没好好看看这位先生，甚至不知道他的名字，而他已经付了一周租金并安顿下来了。

===Original===
Aubrey divined the cause of her hesitation, and gave her his business card.

===Chinese===
奥布里猜到了她犹豫的原因，给了她一张名片。

===Original===
“All right, Mr. Gilbert,” she said. “I’ll send up the girl with some clean towels and a latchkey.”

===Chinese===
“好的，吉尔伯特先生，”她说。“我会让女孩送些干净毛巾和门钥匙上来。”

===Original===
Aubrey sat down in a rocking chair by the window, tucked the muslin curtain to one side, and looked out upon the bright channel of Gissing Street. He was full of the exhilaration that springs from any change of abode, but his romantic satisfaction in being so close to the adorable Titania was somewhat marred by a sense of absurdity, which is feared by young men more than wounds and death. He could see the lighted windows of the Haunted Bookshop quite plainly, but he could not think of any adequate excuse for going over there. And already he realized that to be near Miss Chapman was not at all the consolation he had expected it would be. He had a powerful desire to see her. He turned off the gas, lit his pipe, opened the window, and focused the opera glasses on the door of the bookshop. It brought the place tantalizingly near. He could see the table at the front of the shop, Roger’s bulletin board under the electric light, and one or two nondescript customers gleaning along the shelves. Then something bounded violently under the third button of his shirt. There she was! In the bright, prismatic little circle of the lenses he could see Titania. Heavenly creature, in her white V-necked blouse and brown skirt, there she was looking at a book. He saw her put out one arm and caught the twinkle of her wristwatch. In the startling familiarity of the magnifying glass he could see her bright, unconscious face, the merry profile of her cheek and chin… “The idea of that girl working in a secondhand bookstore!” he exclaimed. “It’s positive sacrilege! Old man Chapman must be crazy.”

===Chinese===
奥布里坐在窗边的摇椅上，把薄纱窗帘拨到一边，眺望着吉辛街明亮的街道。他充满了搬家带来的兴奋感，但如此靠近可爱的蒂塔尼亚的浪漫满足感，却因一种荒谬感而有所减损，这种荒谬感是年轻人比伤口和死亡更害怕的。他能清楚地看到闹鬼书店亮着灯的窗户，但想不出任何去那里的充分借口。他已意识到，接近查普曼小姐（Miss Chapman）完全不是他所期望的慰藉。他强烈地想见到她。他关掉煤气灯，点燃烟斗，打开窗户，将观剧镜对准书店的门。这使那个地方诱人地拉近了。他能看到书店前部的桌子、电灯下的罗杰的布告板，以及一两个在书架旁闲逛的普通顾客。然后，他衬衫第三颗纽扣下有什么东西剧烈地跳动。她在那儿！在明亮、棱镜般的小圆圈中，他看到了蒂塔尼亚。天仙般的可人儿，穿着白色V领衬衫和棕色裙子，她正在看一本书。他看到她伸出一只手臂，瞥见了她手表的闪光。在放大镜惊人的近距离中，他看到了她明亮、无意识的脸庞，她脸颊和下巴欢快的侧影……“让那个女孩在旧书店工作！”他惊呼。“简直是亵渎！查普曼老头肯定疯了。”

===Original===
He took out his pyjamas and threw them on the bed; put his toothbrush and razor on the washbasin, laid hairbrushes and O. Henry on the bureau. Feeling rather seriocomic he loaded his small revolver and hipped it. It was six o’clock, and he wound his watch. He was a little uncertain what to do: whether to keep a vigil at the window with the opera glasses, or go down in the street where he could watch the bookshop more nearly. In the excitement of the adventure he had forgotten all about the cut on his scalp, and felt quite chipper. In leaving Madison Avenue he had attempted to excuse the preposterousness of his excursion by thinking that a quiet weekend in Brooklyn would give him an opportunity to jot down some tentative ideas for Daintybits advertising copy which he planned to submit to his chief on Monday. But now that he was here he felt the impossibility of attacking any such humdrum task. How could he sit down in cold blood to devise any “attention-compelling” layouts for Daintybits Tapioca and Chapman’s Cherished Saratoga Chips, when the daintiest bit of all was only a few yards away? For the first time was made plain to him the amazing power of young women to interfere with the legitimate commerce of the world. He did get so far as to take out his pad of writing paper and jot down

===Chinese===
他拿出睡衣扔在床上；把牙刷和剃须刀放在洗脸台上，把发刷和欧·亨利文集放在五斗柜上。感到有些哭笑不得，他给小左轮手枪装上子弹，别在腰间。六点了，他给表上发条。他有点不确定该做什么：是拿着观剧镜在窗边守夜，还是走到街上能更近地监视书店。在冒险的兴奋中，他完全忘记了头皮上的伤口，感觉相当精神。离开麦迪逊大道时，他试图为这次荒谬的远足找借口，想着在布鲁克林安静地度周末会给他机会记下一些为Daintybits广告文案准备的初步想法，他计划周一提交给上司。但现在他在这里，感到无法进行如此枯燥的任务。当最精致的一口（daintiest bit）只有几码远时，他怎么能冷静地为Daintybits木薯淀粉（Daintybits Tapioca）和查普曼珍藏萨拉托加薯片（Chapman's Cherished Saratoga Chips）设计任何“引人注目”的版面？他第一次清楚地认识到年轻女性干扰世界正常商业的惊人力量。他确实拿出了信纸便签簿，记下了

===Original===
These delicate wafers, crisped by a secret process, cherish in their unique tang and flavour all the life-giving nutriment that has made the potato the King of Vegetables—

===Chinese===
这些精致的薄片，通过秘法制成，在其独特的风味中珍藏着使土豆成为蔬菜之王的所有赋予生命的营养——

===Original===
But the face of Miss Titania kept coming between his hand and brain. Of what avail to flood the world with Chapman Chips if the girl herself should come to any harm? “Was this the face that launched a thousand chips?” he murmured, and for an instant wished he had brought The Oxford Book of English Verse instead of O. Henry.

===Chinese===
但蒂塔尼亚小姐的脸庞不断出现在他的手和大脑之间。如果女孩本人受到伤害，让查普曼薯片（Chapman Chips）充斥世界又有什么用？“就是这张脸让一千片薯片启航了吗？”他喃喃自语，有那么一瞬间希望自己带的是《牛津英语诗选》（The Oxford Book of English Verse）而不是欧·亨利。

===Original===
A tap sounded at his door, and Mrs. Schiller appeared. “Telephone for you, Mr. Gilbert,” she said.

===Chinese===
有人敲了敲他的门，施勒太太出现了。“有你的电话，吉尔伯特先生，”她说。

===Original===
“For me?” said Aubrey in amazement. How could it be for him, he thought, for no one knew he was there.

===Chinese===
“给我？”奥布里惊讶地说。他想，怎么会是给他的，因为没人知道他在这里。

===Original===
“The party on the wire asked to speak to the gentleman who arrived about half an hour ago, and I guess you must be the one he means.”

===Chinese===
“电话那头的人要求和大约半小时前到的那位先生说话，我想他指的一定是你。”

===Original===
“Did he say who he is?” asked Aubrey.

===Chinese===
“他说自己是谁了吗？”奥布里问。

===Original===
“No, sir.”

===Chinese===
“没有，先生。”

===Original===
For a moment Aubrey thought of refusing to answer the call. Then it occurred to him that this would arouse Mrs. Schiller’s suspicions. He ran down to the telephone, which stood under the stairs in the front hall.

===Chinese===
有一刻，奥布里想拒绝接听。但转念一想，这会引起施勒太太的怀疑。他跑下楼去接电话，电话在前厅楼梯下。

===Original===
“Hello,” he said.

===Chinese===
“喂，”他说。

===Original===
“Is this the new guest?” said a voice—a deep, gargling kind of voice.

===Chinese===
“是新来的客人吗？”一个声音说——一种低沉、咯咯作响的声音。

===Original===
“Yes,” said Aubrey.

===Chinese===
“是的，”奥布里说。

===Original===
“Is this the gentleman that arrived half an hour ago with a handbag?”

===Chinese===
“是半小时前提着手提箱到的那位先生吗？”

===Original===
“Yes; who are you?”

===Chinese===
“是的；你是谁？”

===Original===
“I’m a friend,” said the voice; “I wish you well.”

===Chinese===
“我是朋友，”那个声音说；“我祝你好。”

===Original===
“How do you do, friend and well-wisher,” said Aubrey genially.

===Chinese===
“你好，朋友和祝福者（friend and well-wisher），”奥布里亲切地说。

===Original===
“I schust want to warn you that Gissing Street is not healthy for you,” said the voice.

===Chinese===
“我只是想警告你，吉辛街对你来说不健康，”那个声音说。

===Original===
“Is that so?” said Aubrey sharply. “Who are you?”

===Chinese===
“是这样吗？”奥布里厉声说。“你是谁？”

===Original===
“I am a friend,” buzzed the receiver. There was a harsh, bass note in the voice that made the diaphragm at Aubrey’s ear vibrate tinnily. Aubrey grew angry.

===Chinese===
“我是朋友，”听筒里嗡嗡作响。那个声音里有一种刺耳的低音，使奥布里耳朵里的膜片发出金属般的振动。奥布里生气了。

===Original===
“Well, Herr Freund,” he said, “if you’re the well-wisher I met on the bridge last night, watch your step. I’ve got your number.”

===Chinese===
“好吧，弗罗因德先生（Herr Freund），”他说，“如果你是昨晚在桥上遇到的那个祝福者，小心点。我已经认出你了。”

===Original===
There was a pause. Then the other repeated, ponderously, “I am a friend. Gissing Street is not healthy for you.” There was a click, and he had rung off.

===Chinese===
一阵停顿。然后对方沉重地重复道：“我是朋友。吉辛街对你来说不健康。”咔嗒一声，他挂断了电话。

===Original===
Aubrey was a good deal perplexed. He returned to his room, and sat in the dark by the window, smoking a pipe and thinking, with his eyes on the bookshop.

===Chinese===
奥布里非常困惑。他回到房间，在黑暗中坐在窗边，抽着烟斗思考，眼睛盯着书店。

===Original===
There was no longer any doubt in his mind that something sinister was afoot. He reviewed in memory the events of the past few days.

===Chinese===
他心中不再怀疑，有什么邪恶的事情正在进行。他回忆起过去几天的事件。

===Original===
It was on Monday that a bookloving friend had first told him of the existence of the shop on Gissing Street. On Tuesday evening he had gone round to visit the place, and had stayed to supper with Mr. Mifflin. On Wednesday and Thursday he had been busy at the office, and the idea of an intensive Daintybit campaign in Brooklyn had occurred to him. On Friday he had dined with Mr. Chapman, and had run into a curious string of coincidences. He tabulated them:—

===Chinese===
周一，一个爱书的朋友第一次告诉他吉辛街有家书店的存在。周二晚上，他去参观了那家店，并留下来和米夫林先生共进晚餐。周三和周四，他一直在办公室忙，布鲁克林Daintybit强化营销活动的点子在他脑海中浮现。周五，他与查普曼先生共进晚餐，遇到了一连串奇特的巧合。他列出了它们：——

===Original===
1. The Lost ad in the Times on Friday morning.
2. The chef in the elevator carrying the book that was supposed to be lost—he being the same man Aubrey had seen in the bookshop on Tuesday evening.
3. Seeing the chef again on Gissing Street.
4. The return of the book to the bookshop.
5. Mifflin had said that the book had been stolen from him. Then why should it be either advertised or returned?
6. The rebinding of the book.
7. Finding the original cover of the book in Weintraub’s drug store.
8. The affair on the Bridge.
9. The telephone message from “a friend”—a friend with an obviously Teutonic voice.

===Chinese===
1. 周五早上《时报》上的失物广告。
2. 电梯里拿着那本据称丢失的书的厨师——他就是奥布里周二晚上在书店看到的那个人。
3. 在吉辛街上再次看到那个厨师。
4. 书被送回书店。
5. 米夫林曾说这本书是被从他那里偷走的。那么为什么它会被登广告或送回？
6. 书被重新装订。
7. 在温特劳布药店发现了书的原始封面。
8. 桥上事件。
9. 来自“朋友”的电话留言——一个明显带有条顿口音的朋友。

===Original===
He remembered the face of anger and fear displayed by the Octagon chef when he had spoken to him in the elevator. Until this oddly menacing telephone message, he could have explained the attack on the Bridge as merely a haphazard footpad enterprise; but now he was forced to conclude that it was in some way connected with his visits to the bookshop. He felt, too, that in some unknown way Weintraub’s drug store had something to do with it. Would he have been attacked if he had not taken the book cover from the drug store? He got the cover out of his bag and looked at it again. It was of plain blue cloth, with the title stamped in gold on the back, and at the bottom the lettering London: Chapman and Hall. From the width of the backstrap it was evident that the book had been a fat one. Inside the front cover the figure 60 was written in red pencil—this he took to be Roger Mifflin’s price mark. Inside the back cover he found the following notations—

===Chinese===
他记得在电梯里与那位八角饭店厨师说话时，对方脸上表现出的愤怒和恐惧。直到这个奇怪而威胁的电话留言之前，他还可以将桥上的袭击解释为仅仅是偶然的拦路抢劫；但现在他被迫得出结论，这与他造访书店在某种程度上有关。他也觉得，温特劳布药店以某种未知的方式与这事有关。如果他没有从药店拿走书的封面，他会受到袭击吗？他从包里拿出封面，再次端详。它是朴素的蓝色布面，书名烫金印在背面，底部印有伦敦：查普曼和霍尔（Chapman and Hall）字样。从书脊带的宽度可以明显看出，这是一本厚书。在封面内页，用红铅笔写着数字60——他认为这是罗杰·米夫林的标价。在封底内页，他发现了以下标注——

===Original===
vol. 3—166, 174, 210, 329, 349
329 ff. cf. W. W.

===Chinese===
第3卷——166、174、210、329、349
329页以下 参见 W. W.

===Original===
These references were written in black ink, in a small, neat hand. Below them, in quite a different script and in pale violet ink, was written

===Chinese===
这些参考文献是用黑色墨水写成的，字迹小巧工整。在它们下方，用完全不同的笔迹和淡紫色墨水写着

===Original===
153 (3) 1, 2

===Chinese===
153 (3) 1, 2

===Original===
“I suppose these are page numbers,” Aubrey thought. “I think I’d better have a look at that book.”

===Chinese===
“我想这些是页码，”奥布里想。“我想我最好看看那本书。”

===Original===
He put the cover in his pocket and went out for a bite of supper. “It’s a puzzle with three sides to it,” he thought, as he descended the crepitant stairs, “The Bookshop, the Octagon, and Weintraub’s; but that book seems to be the clue to the whole business.”

===Chinese===
他把封面放进口袋，出去吃点晚饭。“这是个有三面的谜题，”他走下吱嘎作响的楼梯时想，“书店、八角饭店和温特劳布药店；但那本书似乎是整个事件的线索。”

## VIII Aubrey Goes to the Movies, and Wishes He Knew More German / VIII 奥布里看电影，后悔德语没多学一点

===Original===
A few doors from the bookshop was a small lunchroom named after the great city of Milwaukee, one of those pleasant refectories where the diner buys his food at the counter and eats it sitting in a flat-armed chair. Aubrey got a bowl of soup, a cup of coffee, beef stew, and bran muffins, and took them to an empty seat by the window. He ate with one eye on the street. From his place in the corner he could command the strip of pavement in front of Mifflin's shop. Halfway through the stew he saw Roger come out onto the pavement and begin to remove the books from the boxes.

After finishing his supper he lit one of his "mild but they satisfy" cigarettes and sat in the comfortable warmth of a nearby radiator. A large black cat lay sprawled on the next chair. Up at the service counter there was a pleasant clank of stout crockery as occasional customers came in and ordered their victuals. Aubrey began to feel a relaxation swim through his veins. Gissing Street was very bright and orderly in its Saturday evening bustle. Certainly it was grotesque to imagine melodrama hanging about a secondhand bookshop in Brooklyn. The revolver felt absurdly lumpy and uncomfortable in his hip pocket. What a different aspect a little hot supper gives to affairs! The most resolute idealist or assassin had better write his poems or plan his atrocities before the evening meal. After the narcosis of that repast the spirit falls into a softer mood, eager only to be amused. Even Milton would hardly have had the inhuman fortitude to sit down to the manuscript of *Paradise Lost* right after supper. Aubrey began to wonder if his unpleasant suspicions had not been overdrawn. He thought how delightful it would be to stop in at the bookshop and ask Titania to go to the movies with him.

Curious magic of thought! The idea was still sparkling in his mind when he saw Titania and Mrs. Mifflin emerge from the bookshop and pass briskly in front of the lunchroom. They were talking and laughing merrily. Titania's face, shining with young vitality, seemed to him more "attention-compelling" than any ten-point Caslon type-arrangement he had ever seen. He admired the layout of her face from the standpoint of his cherished technique. "Just enough 'white space,' " he thought, "to set off her eyes as the 'centre of interest.' Her features aren't this modern boldface stuff, set solid," he said to himself, thinking typographically. "They're rather French old-style italic, slightly leaded. Set on 22-point body, I guess. Old man Chapman's a pretty good typefounder, you have to hand it to him."

He smiled at this conceit, seized hat and coat, and dashed out of the lunchroom.

===Chinese===
书店隔几家门面有家小餐馆，以密尔沃基（Milwaukee）这座伟大的城市命名，是那种让人愉快的简餐铺子——食客在柜台买好食物，端到扶手椅上坐下来吃。奥布里（Aubrey Gilbert）要了一碗汤、一杯咖啡、一份炖牛肉和麸皮松饼，端到窗边一个空位上坐下。他一边吃，一边留意着街上的动静。坐在角落里，他恰好能望见米夫林（Roger Mifflin）书店门前的人行道。炖牛肉吃到一半时，他看见罗杰走到人行道上，开始把书从箱子里搬出来。

吃完晚饭，他点了一支"温和但过瘾"牌香烟，坐到附近暖气片旁暖洋洋的舒适角落里。一只大黑猫摊开四肢趴在隔壁椅子上。柜台那边不时传来厚实瓷器碰撞的愉快叮当声，偶尔有顾客进来点餐。奥布里觉得一股松弛感涌遍了全身。吉辛街（Gissing Street）周六傍晚的热闹景象明亮而有序。想象布鲁克林一家二手书店周围有什么惊险剧码，未免太荒唐了。那把左轮手枪在后裤兜里硌得又鼓又不舒服，简直可笑。一顿热腾腾的晚饭能把事情的面目改变得多大啊！最坚定的理想主义者也好，最冷酷的刺客也好，最好都在晚饭前写诗或者策划暴行。饱餐之后的麻醉感会让灵魂陷入一种更柔软的情绪，只想找点乐子。就连弥尔顿（Milton）恐怕也没有那种非人的意志力，会在晚饭后立刻坐下来写《失乐园》（*Paradise Lost*）的手稿。奥布里开始怀疑自己那些令人不快的疑心是不是过了头。他想，要是此刻走进书店，邀请蒂塔尼亚（Titania Chapman）一起去看电影，该多好啊。

念头真有奇妙的魔力！这个想法还在他脑中闪闪发光时，他就看见蒂塔尼亚和米夫林太太（Helen Mifflin）从书店里走出来，轻快地经过餐馆门前。两人有说有笑，十分开心。蒂塔尼亚的脸庞焕发着年轻的光彩，在奥布里看来，比他见过的任何十点卡斯隆字体排版都更"引人注目"。他用自己心爱的排印技术审视着她面容的布局。"恰到好处的'留白'，"他想，"把她的眼睛衬托成'视觉焦点'。她的五官不是那种密密实实排满的现代粗体字，"他用排版术语自言自语，"倒像是法式老式斜体，行距还稍微宽了些。我猜是22磅的正文。查普曼（Mr. Chapman）老头算是个相当不错的铸字匠，这一点得承认。"

他被自己这番异想天开逗笑了，抓起帽子和外套冲出了餐馆。

===Original===
Mrs. Mifflin and Titania had halted a few yards up the street, and were looking at some pert little bonnets in a window. Aubrey hurried across the street, ran up to the next corner, recrossed, and walked down the eastern pavement. In this way he would meet them as though he were coming from the subway. He felt rather more excited than King Albert reentering Brussels. He saw them coming, chattering together in the delightful fashion of women out on a spree. Helen seemed much younger in the company of her companion. "A lining of pussy-willow taffeta and an embroidered slip-on," she was saying.

Aubrey steered onto them with an admirable gesture of surprise.

"Well, I never!" said Mrs. Mifflin. "Here's Mr. Gilbert. Were you coming to see Roger?" she added, rather enjoying the young man's predicament.

Titania shook hands cordially. Aubrey, searching the old-style italics with the desperate intensity of a proofreader, saw no evidence of chagrin at seeing him again so soon.

"Why," he said rather lamely, "I was coming to see you all. I—I wondered how you were getting along."

Mrs. Mifflin had pity on him. "We've left Mr. Mifflin to look after the shop," she said. "He's busy with some of his old crony customers. Why don't you come with us to the movies?"

"Yes, do," said Titania. "It's Mr. and Mrs. Sidney Drew, you know how adorable they are!"

No one needs to be told how quickly Aubrey assented. Pleasure coincided with duty in that the outer wing of the party placed him next to Titania.

"Well, how do you like bookselling?" he asked.

"Oh, it's the greatest fun!" she cried. "But it'll take me ever and ever so long to learn about all the books. People ask such questions! A woman came in this afternoon looking for a copy of *Blasé Tales*. How was I to know she wanted *The Blazed Trail*?"

"You'll get used to that," said Mrs. Mifflin. "Just a minute, people, I want to stop in at the drug store."

They went into Weintraub's pharmacy. Entranced as he was by the proximity of Miss Chapman, Aubrey noticed that the druggist eyed him rather queerly. And being of a noticing habit, he also observed that when Weintraub had occasion to write out a label for a box of powdered alum Mrs. Mifflin was buying, he did so with a pale violet ink.

===Chinese===
米夫林太太和蒂塔尼亚在街上走了几码远停下来，正在一家橱窗前看几顶俏皮的小帽子。奥布里急忙穿过街道，跑到下一个街角，再穿回来，沿着东侧人行道往下走。这样一来，他遇见她们时就好像是从地铁过来的。他此刻的兴奋劲儿，简直比回到布鲁塞尔的阿尔贝国王（King Albert）还要强烈。他看见她们迎面走来，兴高采烈地聊着天，那是女人出门闲逛时特有的欢快模样。海伦（Helen）在同伴的衬托下显得年轻了许多。"衬里用猫柳塔夫绸，再配上一只绣花套鞋，"她正说着。

奥布里巧妙地装出一副惊喜的样子迎了上去。

"哎呀，真没想到！"米夫林太太说，"是吉尔伯特先生啊。你是来找罗杰的吧？"她又补了一句，颇有些乐见这个年轻人的窘态。

蒂塔尼亚热情地和他握了手。奥布里用校对员那种拼命的劲头审视着那副"法式老式斜体"，没有发现任何因为这么快又见面而懊恼的迹象。

"呃，"他相当笨拙地说，"我是来看你们大家的。我——我想知道你们过得怎么样。"

米夫林太太对他起了怜悯之心。"我们把米夫林先生留在店里看店了，"她说，"他正忙着应付几个老主顾。你何不跟我们一起去看电影？"

"对，一起来吧，"蒂塔尼亚说，"是西德尼·德鲁（Sidney Drew）先生和太太的片子，你知道他们有多可爱！"

不用说，奥布里答应得有多快。愉悦与使命不谋而合——一行人走在街上，外侧的位置刚好把他安排在蒂塔尼亚旁边。

"你喜欢卖书这行吗？"他问道。

"哦，太好玩了！"她叫道，"可是要把所有的书都搞明白，得花好长好长的时间。人们提的问题千奇百怪！今天下午有位女士来找一本《厌世故事》（*Blasé Tales*），我怎么知道她其实是想要《火燎小径》（*The Blazed Trail*）呢？"

"你会习惯的，"米夫林太太说，"等一下，各位，我要去药店买点东西。"

她们走进了温特劳布（Weintraub）的药店。查普曼小姐近在咫尺，奥布里虽然心醉神迷，却注意到药剂师看他的眼神有些古怪。他素有观察的习惯，还注意到温特劳布给米夫林太太买的一盒明矾粉写标签时，用的是淡紫色墨水。

===Original===
At the glass sentry-box in front of the theatre Aubrey insisted on buying the tickets.

"We came out right after supper," said Titania as they entered, "so as to get in before the crowd."

It is not so easy, however, to get ahead of Brooklyn movie fans. They had to stand for several minutes in a packed lobby while a stern young man held the waiting crowd in check with a velvet rope. Aubrey sustained delightful spasms of the protective instinct in trying to shelter Titania from buffets and pushings. Unknown to her, his arm extended behind her like an iron rod to absorb the onward impulses of the eager throng. A rustling groan ran through these enthusiasts as they saw the preliminary footage of the great *Tarzan* flash onto the screen, and realized they were missing something. At last, however, the trio got through the barrier and found three seats well in front, at one side. From this angle the flying pictures were strangely distorted, but Aubrey did not mind.

"Isn't it lucky I got here when I did," whispered Titania. "Mr. Mifflin has just had a telephone call from Philadelphia asking him to go over on Monday to make an estimate on a library that's going to be sold so I'll be able to look after the shop for him while he's gone."

"Is that so?" said Aubrey. "Well, now, I've got to be in Brooklyn on Monday, on business. Maybe Mrs. Mifflin would let me come in and buy some books from you."

"Customers always welcome," said Mrs. Mifflin.

"I've taken a fancy to that *Cromwell* book," said Aubrey. "What do you suppose Mr. Mifflin would sell it for?"

"I think that book must be valuable," said Titania. "Somebody came in this afternoon and wanted to buy it, but Mr. Mifflin wouldn't part with it. He says it's one of his favourites. Gracious, what a weird film this is!"

The fantastic absurdities of Tarzan proceeded on the screen, tearing celluloid passions to tatters, but Aubrey found the strong man of the jungle coming almost too close to his own imperious instincts. Was not he, too—he thought naively—a poor Tarzan of the advertising jungle, lost among the elephants and alligators of commerce, and sighing for this dainty and unattainable vision of girlhood that had burst upon his burning gaze! He stole a perilous side-glance at her profile, and saw the racing flicker of the screen reflected in tiny spangles of light that danced in her eyes. He was even so unknowing as to imagine that she was not aware of his contemplation. And then the lights went up.

===Chinese===
在剧院门前的玻璃亭里，奥布里坚持要买票。

"我们吃完饭就出来了，"蒂塔尼亚一边进场一边说，"好赶在人群前面进去。"

然而，要赶在布鲁克林的影迷前面可没那么容易。他们不得不在拥挤的大厅里站了好几分钟，一个表情严肃的年轻人用天鹅绒绳索维持着排队秩序。奥布里享受着保护欲带来的愉悦刺痛——他试图替蒂塔尼亚挡住推搡和碰撞。蒂塔尼亚浑然不知，他的手臂像铁杆一样挡在她身后，替她挡住人群向前涌的推力。当大银幕上闪出伟大的《人猿泰山》（*Tarzan*）的片头影像，观众们意识到自己错过了什么，一阵低沉的叹息声在影迷中蔓延开来。不过最终三人还是挤过了隔离带，在前排一侧找到了三个座位。从这个角度看上去，银幕上的画面严重变形，但奥布里毫不在意。

"我来得可真是时候，"蒂塔尼亚低声说，"米夫林先生刚接到费城来的电话，请他周一过去估价一批要拍卖的藏书，到时候我可以替他看店。"

"是吗？"奥布里说，"嘿，我周一恰好要到布鲁克林来办事。说不定米夫林太太会允许我过来，从你这儿买几本书。"

"随时欢迎顾客光临，"米夫林太太说。

"我看上了那本《克伦威尔》（*Cromwell*），"奥布里说，"你猜米夫林先生会卖多少钱？"

"我觉得那本书一定很值钱，"蒂塔尼亚说，"今天下午有人来想买，但米夫林先生不肯出手。他说那是他的心头好之一。天哪，这电影真够怪的！"

《人猿泰山》的荒诞奇景在银幕上继续上演，把赛璐珞上的激情撕成碎片，但奥布里觉得丛林之王那种蛮横的本能几乎太接近他自己的内心了。他不也——他天真地想——是一个广告丛林里可怜的泰山，迷失在商业世界的象群和鳄鱼之间，为这清丽而遥不可及的少女倩影叹息不已吗！他偷偷侧目瞥了她一眼，看到银幕上飞速闪动的光影化作细碎的亮斑，在她眼眸中闪烁。他甚至天真地以为她没有察觉到他的注视。就在这时，灯光亮了。

===Original===
"What nonsense, wasn't it?" said Titania. "I'm so glad it's over! I was quite afraid one of those elephants would walk off the screen and tread on us."

"I never can understand," said Helen, "why they don't film some of the really good books—think of Frank Stockton's stuff, how delightful that would be. Can't you imagine Mr. and Mrs. Drew playing in *Rudder Grange*!"

"Thank goodness!" said Titania. "Since I entered the book business, that's the first time anybody's mentioned a book that I've read. Yes—do you remember when Pomona and Jonas visit an insane asylum on their honeymoon? Do you know, you and Mr. Mifflin remind me a little of Mr. and Mrs. Drew."

Helen and Aubrey chuckled at this innocent correlation of ideas. Then the organ began to play "O How I Hate To Get Up in the Morning" and the ever-delightful Mr. and Mrs. Drew appeared on the screen in one of their domestic comedies. Lovers of the movies may well date a new screen era from the day those whimsical pantomimers set their wholesome and humane talent at the service of the arc light and the lens. Aubrey felt a serene and intimate pleasure in watching them from a seat beside Titania. He knew that the breakfast table scene shadowed before them was only a makeshift section of lath propped up in some barnlike motion picture studio; yet his rocketing fancy imagined it as some arcadian suburb where he and Titania, by a jugglery of benign fate, were bungalo wed together. Young men have a pioneering imagination: it is doubtful whether any young Orlando ever found himself side by side with Rosalind without dreaming himself wedded to her. If men die a thousand deaths before this mortal coil is shuffled, even so surely do youths contract a thousand marriages before they go to the City Hall for a license.

Aubrey remembered the opera glasses, which were still in his pocket, and brought them out. The trio amused themselves by watching Sidney Drew's face through the magnifying lenses. They were disappointed in the result, however, as the pictures, when so enlarged, revealed all the cobweb of fine cracks on the film. Mr. Drew's nose, the most amusing feature known to the movies, lost its quaintness when so augmented.

"Why," cried Titania, "it makes his lovely nose look like the map of Florida."

"How on earth did you happen to have these in your pocket?" asked Mrs. Mifflin, returning the glasses.

Aubrey was hard pressed for a prompt and reasonable fib, but advertising men are resourceful.

"Oh," he said, "I sometimes carry them with me at night to study the advertising sky-signs. I'm a little short sighted. You see, it's part of my business to study the technique of the electric signs."

===Chinese===
"胡说八道，不是吗？"蒂塔尼亚说，"可算演完了，太好了！我还真怕有一头大象从银幕上走下来踩我们呢。"

"我真搞不懂，"海伦说，"他们为什么不拍些真正好的书——想想弗兰克·斯托克顿（Frank Stockton）的作品，那该多有意思。你想象一下德鲁先生和太太演《船舵农庄》（*Rudder Grange*），多棒！"

"谢天谢地！"蒂塔尼亚说，"自从我入了卖书这行，还是头一回有人提起一本我读过的书。是啊——你还记得波莫纳（Pomona）和乔纳斯（Jonas）蜜月期间去精神病院参观那章吗？你知道吗，你和米夫林先生让我有点想起德鲁先生和太太。"

海伦和奥布里被这个天真的联想逗得咯咯笑了。接着风琴响起来，奏起《我多么讨厌早起》（"O How I Hate To Get Up in the Morning"），永远令人愉快的德鲁夫妇出现在银幕上，演的是一部家庭喜剧。电影爱好者们完全可以把这一天视为银幕新时代的起点——从那天起，这两位妙趣横生的哑剧演员将他们健康而人道的才华奉献给了弧光灯和镜头。坐在蒂塔尼亚身旁看着他们，奥布里感到一种宁静而亲密的愉悦。他知道眼前上演的早餐桌场景不过是谷仓般的电影摄影棚里搭起来的一块薄板布景；然而他飞驰的想象却把它当作某个田园牧歌式的郊区——在那里，他和蒂塔尼亚，被仁慈命运的戏法撮合在一起，住在同一栋平房里。年轻人有的是开拓性的想象力：很难说有哪个年轻的奥兰多（Orlando）不曾梦想着娶罗莎琳德（Rosalind）为妻。如果说男人在这具凡躯入土之前要死上一千次，那么年轻人去市政厅领结婚证之前，同样会缔结一千次姻缘。

奥布里想起歌剧望远镜还在口袋里，便掏了出来。三人透过放大镜看西德尼·德鲁的脸，觉得很好玩。不过结果让他们失望了，因为画面放这么大之后，胶片上蛛网般的细小裂纹全都暴露无遗。德鲁先生的鼻子——电影界最出名的滑稽特征——放大后反而失去了它那股古灵精怪的味道。

"哎呀，"蒂塔尼亚叫道，"把他的漂亮鼻子弄得跟佛罗里达地图似的。"

"你口袋里怎么会有这种东西？"米夫林太太一边把望远镜递回去一边问。

奥布里一时编不出一个既快又像样的谎话，不过广告人嘛，总归是有办法的。

"哦，"他说，"我晚上出门有时会带着它，用来研究霓虹灯广告牌。我有点近视。你知道，研究电灯广告牌的技术，算是我工作的一部分。"

===Original===
After some current event pictures the programme prepared to repeat itself, and they went out. "Will you come in and have some cocoa with us?" said Helen as they reached the door of the bookshop. Aubrey was eager enough to accept, but feared to overplay his hand. "I'm sorry," he said, "but I think I'd better not. I've got some work to do tonight. Perhaps I can drop in on Monday when Mr. Mifflin's away, and put coal on the furnace for you, or something of that sort?"

Mrs. Mifflin laughed. "Surely!" she said. "You're welcome any time." The door closed behind them, and Aubrey fell into a profound melancholy. Deprived of the heavenly rhetoric of her eye, Gissing Street seemed flat and dull.

It was still early—not quite ten o'clock—and it occurred to Aubrey that if he was going to patrol the neighbourhood he had better fix its details in his head. Hazlitt, the next street below the bookshop, proved to be a quiet little byway, cheerfully lit with modest dwellings. A few paces down Hazlitt Street a narrow cobbled alley ran through to Wordsworth Avenue, passing between the back yards of Gissing Street and Whittier Street. The alley was totally dark, but by counting off the correct number of houses Aubrey identified the rear entrance of the bookshop. He tried the yard gate cautiously, and found it unlocked. Glancing in he could see a light in the kitchen window and assumed that the cocoa was being brewed. Then a window glowed upstairs, and he was thrilled to see Titania shining in the lamplight. She moved to the window and pulled down the blind. For a moment he saw her head and shoulders silhouetted against the curtain; then the light went out.

Aubrey stood briefly in sentimental thought. If he only had a couple of blankets, he mused, he could camp out here in Roger's back yard all night. Surely no harm could come to the girl while he kept watch beneath her casement! The idea was just fantastic enough to appeal to him. Then, as he stood in the open gateway, he heard distant footfalls coming down the alley, and a grumble of voices. Perhaps two policemen on their rounds, he thought: it would be awkward to be surprised skulking about back doors at this time of night. He slipped inside the gate and closed it gently behind him, taking the precaution to slip the bolt.

===Chinese===
几则时事短片放完之后，节目准备从头再演一遍，他们便退了场。走到书店门口时，海伦说："进来喝杯可可好吗？"奥布里巴不得接受，但又怕演过头了。"抱歉，"他说，"我想还是不了。今晚还有些工作要做。或许周一米夫林先生出门的时候，我可以过来，帮你往炉子里添煤什么的？"

米夫林太太笑了。"当然可以！"她说，"随时欢迎。"门在他们身后关上了，奥布里陷入深深的忧郁之中。失去了她那双明眸的天赐修辞，吉辛街变得平淡而乏味。

时间还早——还不到十点——奥布里想到，既然要巡逻这一带，最好先把地形细节记在脑子里。书店下面那条哈兹利特街（Hazlitt Street）是条安静的小岔路，两边是朴素的住宅，灯光柔和。沿着哈兹利特街走几步，有一条鹅卵石铺的窄巷通向华兹华斯大道（Wordsworth Avenue），从吉辛街和惠蒂尔街（Whittier Street）的后院之间穿过。巷子里一片漆黑，但奥布里数着门牌号，辨认出了书店的后门。他小心翼翼地试了试院子的门，发现没有上锁。往里一看，厨房窗户里透着灯光，大概是正在煮可可。接着楼上一扇窗户亮了起来，他激动地看到蒂塔尼亚在灯光中闪现的身影。她走到窗前，拉下了百叶窗。有那么一瞬间，他看见她的头部和肩膀映在窗帘上形成剪影；随后灯灭了。

奥布里站在那儿伤感了片刻。他想，要是有两条毯子就好了，可以在罗杰家的后院露营一整夜。有他在她的窗下守着，这姑娘肯定不会出什么事的！这个想法虽然离奇，但正合他的胃口。就在他站在敞开的门洞里时，他听到远处传来巷子里的脚步声，还有低沉的说话声。他想，也许是两个巡逻的警察：深更半夜在人家后门口晃悠，被撞见可就尴尬了。他溜进门去，轻轻把门带上，还小心地插上了门闩。

===Original===
The footsteps came nearer, stumbling down the uneven cobbles in the darkness. He stood still against the back fence. To his amazement the men halted outside Mifflin's gate, and he heard the latch quietly lifted.

"It's no use," said a voice—"the gate is locked. We must find some other way, my friend."

Aubrey tingled to hear the rolling, throaty *r* in the last word. There was no mistaking—this was the voice of his "friend and well-wisher" over the telephone.

The other said something in German in a hoarse whisper. Having studied that language in college, Aubrey caught only two words—*Thür* and *Schlüssel*, which he knew meant "door" and "key."

"Very well," said the first voice. "That will be all right, but we must act tonight. The damned thing must be finished tomorrow. Your idiotic stupidity—"

Again followed some gargling in German, in a rapid undertone too fluent for Aubrey's grasp. The latch of the alley gate clicked once more, and his hand was on his revolver; but in a moment the two had passed on down the alley.

The young advertising agent stood against the fence in silent horror, his heart bumping heavily. His hands were clammy, his feet seemed to have grown larger and taken root. What damnable complot was this? A sultry wave of anger passed over him. This bland, slick, talkative bookseller, was he arranging some blackmailing scheme to kidnap the girl and wring blood-money out of her father? And in league with Germans, too, the scoundrel! What an asinine thing for old Chapman to send an unprotected girl over here into the wilds of Brooklyn… and in the meantime, what was he to do? Patrol the back yard all night? No, the friend and well-wisher had said "We must find some other way." Besides, Aubrey remembered something having been said about the old terrier sleeping in the kitchen. He felt sure Bock would not let any German in at night without raising the roof. Probably the best way would be to watch the front of the shop. In miserable perplexity he waited several minutes until the two Germans would be well out of earshot. Then he unbolted the gate and stole up the alley on tiptoe, in the opposite direction. It led into Wordsworth Avenue just behind Weintraub's drug store, over the rear of which hung the great girders and trestles of the "L" station, a kind of Swiss chalet straddling the street on stilts. He thought it prudent to make a detour, so he turned east on Wordsworth Avenue until he reached Whittier Street, then sauntered easily down Whittier for a block, spying sharply for evidences of pursuit. Brooklyn was putting out its lights for the night, and all was quiet. He turned into Hazlitt Street and so back onto Gissing, noticing now that the Haunted Bookshop lights were off. It was nearly eleven o'clock: the last audience was filing out of the movie theatre, where two workmen were already perched on ladders taking down the *Tarzan* electric light sign, to substitute the illuminated lettering for the next feature.

===Chinese===
脚步声越来越近，在黑暗中深一脚浅一脚地踩在高低不平的鹅卵石上。他一动不动地贴着后篱笆站着。令他吃惊的是，那两个人在米夫林家的门外停了下来，他听到门闩被轻轻抬起。

"没用的，"一个声音说——"门锁上了。我们得另想办法，我的朋友。"

听到"朋友"这个词里滚动的、从喉间发出的小舌音*r*，奥布里浑身一激灵。没错——这就是电话里那个"好心朋友"的声音。

另一个人用嘶哑的低嗓说了几句德语。奥布里大学里学过这门语言，但只听出了两个词——*Thür*和*Schlüssel*，他知道意思分别是"门"和"钥匙"。

"好吧，"第一个声音说，"那就没问题，但我们今晚必须行动。那该死的东西明天必须完工。你这白痴蠢货——"

又是一通德语的咕哝，语速极快，低沉含混，奥布里听不明白。巷子门的门闩又咔嗒响了一声，他的手已经摸到了左轮手枪；但不一会儿，两个人就沿着巷子走远了。

年轻的广告代理商靠在篱笆上，恐惧无声地攫住了他，心脏沉重地怦怦跳着。他的手心全是汗，双脚仿佛变大了，扎进了地里。这到底是什么该死的阴谋？一股灼热的怒火涌上心头。这个温文尔雅、油嘴滑舌、喋喋不休的书商，他在策划一场绑架勒索的勾当吗——绑架这姑娘，然后从她父亲身上榨出血汗钱？而且还是跟德国人勾结在一起，这恶棍！老查普曼也是昏了头，竟把一个无依无靠的姑娘送到布鲁克林这荒郊野外来……而眼下他该怎么办？整夜在后院巡逻吗？不，"好心朋友"说了"我们得另想办法"。再说了，奥布里记得有人提过，那只老梗犬（terrier）是睡在厨房里的。他相信博克（Bock）不会在夜里放任何德国人进门而不闹翻天的。最好的办法大概还是盯着书店正门。他在痛苦的困惑中等了几分钟，确认两个德国人已经走远、听不见了。然后他拔开门闩，踮着脚尖沿巷子往反方向溜去。巷子通到华兹华斯大道，正好在温特劳布药店后面，药店上方高耸着高架铁路（"L"）车站的钢梁和支架，那座瑞士木屋式的车站像踩着高跷一样横跨在街道上。他觉得还是绕道为妙，于是向东拐上华兹华斯大道，走到惠蒂尔街，再不慌不忙地沿着惠蒂尔街走了一个街区，眼睛尖利地搜寻着是否有人跟踪。布鲁克林正在熄灯歇夜，四下一片寂静。他拐进哈兹利特街，再回到吉辛街，注意到闹鬼书店的灯已经灭了。快十一点了：最后一拨观众正从电影院里鱼贯而出，两个工人已经爬上梯子在拆《人猿泰山》的霓虹灯招牌，换上下一场片子的发光字。

===Original===
After some debate he decided that the best thing to do was to return to his room at Mrs. Schiller's, from which he could keep a sharp watch on the front door of the bookshop. By good fortune there was a lamp post almost directly in front of Mifflin's house, which cast plenty of light on the little sunken area before the door. With his opera glasses he could see from his bedroom whatever went on. As he crossed the street he cast his eyes upward at the façade of Mrs. Schiller's house. Two windows in the fourth storey were lit, and the gas burned minutely in the downstairs hall, elsewhere all was dark. And then, as he glanced at the window of his own chamber, where the curtain was still tucked back behind the pane, he noticed a curious thing. A small point of rosy light glowed, faded, and glowed again by the window. Someone was smoking a cigar in his room.

Aubrey continued walking in even stride, as though he had seen nothing. Returning down the street, on the opposite side, he verified his first glance. The light was still there, and he judged himself not far out in assuming the smoker to be the friend and well-wisher or one of his gang. He had suspected the other man in the alley of being Weintraub, but he could not be sure. A cautious glance through the window of the drugstore revealed Weintraub at his prescription counter. Aubrey determined to get even with the guttural gentleman who was waiting for him, certainly with no affectionate intent. He thanked the good fortune that had led him to stick the book cover in his overcoat pocket when leaving Mrs. Schiller's. Evidently, for reasons unknown, someone was very anxious to get hold of it.

An idea occurred to him as he passed the little florist's shop, which was just closing. He entered and bought a dozen white carnations, and then, as if by an afterthought, asked "Have you any wire?"

The florist produced a spool of the slender, tough wire that is sometimes used to nip the buds of expensive roses, to prevent them from blossoming too quickly.

"Let me have about eight feet," said Aubrey. "I need some tonight and I guess the hardware stores are all closed."

With this he returned to Mrs. Schiller's, picking his way carefully and close to the houses so as to be out of sight from the upstairs windows. He climbed the steps and unlatched the door with bated breath. It was half-past eleven, and he wondered how long he would have to wait for the well-wisher to descend.

===Chinese===
经过一番思量，他认定最好的办法是回到施勒太太（Mrs. Schiller）住处的房间里，从那里可以严密监视书店的正门。幸运的是，米夫林家门前几乎正对着一根路灯柱，把门前那片下沉的小区域照得亮堂堂的。用歌剧望远镜从卧室里就能看清外面发生的一切。过马路时，他抬头望了望施勒太太那栋房子的正面。四楼两扇窗户亮着灯，楼下大厅里的煤气灯只烧着微弱的火苗，其他地方一片漆黑。就在这时，他瞥了一眼自己房间的窗户——窗帘还卷在玻璃后面——注意到一件怪事。窗边有一点玫瑰色的光，亮了，暗了，又亮了。有人在他房间里抽雪茄。

奥布里保持着匀速继续走着，仿佛什么也没看见。他折回街道对面，再次确认了第一眼的判断。那光点还在，他觉得自己猜得不错——那个抽烟的人就是"好心朋友"或者他那伙人的一个。他怀疑巷子里另一个人是温特劳布，但不能确定。他小心翼翼地朝药店里张望了一眼，看到温特劳布在处方柜台后面。奥布里决心要跟这位等着他的、满口喉音的绅士算一笔账，对方显然不怀好意。他暗暗感谢命运的安排，让他出门时把那本书的封皮塞进了大衣口袋。显然，出于某种未知的原因，有人非常急切地想要得到它。

经过那家正在关门的小花店时，他灵机一动。他走进去买了一打白色康乃馨，然后像是突然想起来似的问道："你有铁丝吗？"

花匠拿出一卷又细又韧的铁丝，这种铁丝有时用来掐住名贵玫瑰的花苞，防止它们开得太快。

"给我来八英尺左右，"奥布里说，"今晚急用，我猜五金店都关门了。"

他带着这些回到施勒太太的住处，小心翼翼地紧贴着房屋行走，以免被楼上窗户里的人看见。他登上台阶，屏住呼吸轻轻推开了门。十一点半了，他不知道要等多久"好心朋友"才会下楼。

===Original===
He could not help chuckling as he made his preparations, remembering an occasion at college somewhat similar in setting though far less serious in purpose. First he took off his shoes, laying them carefully to one side where he could find them again in a hurry. Then, choosing a banister about six feet from the bottom of the stairs he attached one end of the wire tightly to its base and spread the slack in a large loop over two of the stair treads. The remaining end of the wire he passed out through the banisters, twisting it into a small loop so that he could pull it easily. Then he turned out the hall gas and sat down in the dark to wait events.

He sat for a long time, in some nervousness lest the pug dog might come prowling and find him. He was startled by a lady in a dressing gown—perhaps Mrs. J. F. Smith—who emerged from a ground-floor room passed very close to him in the dark, and muttered upstairs. He twitched his noose out of the way just in time. Presently, however, his patience was rewarded. He heard a door squeak above, and then the groaning of the staircase as someone descended slowly. He relaid his trap and waited, smiling to himself. A clock somewhere in the house was chiming twelve as the man came groping down the last flight, feeling his way in the dark. Aubrey heard him swearing under his breath.

At the precise moment, when both his victim's feet were within the loop, Aubrey gave the wire a gigantic tug. The man fell like a safe, crashing against the banisters and landing in a sprawl on the floor. It was a terrific fall, and shook the house. He lay there groaning and cursing.

Barely retaining his laughter, Aubrey struck a match and held it over the sprawling figure. The man lay with his face twisted against one outspread arm, but the beard was unmistakable. It was the assistant chef again, and he seemed partly unconscious. "Burnt hair is a grand restorative," said Aubrey to himself, and applied the match to the bush of beard. He singed off a couple of inches of it with intense delight, and laid his carnations on the head of the stricken one. Then, hearing stirrings in the basement, he gathered up his wire and shoes and fled upstairs. He gained his room roaring with inward mirth, but entered cautiously, fearing some trap. Save for a strong tincture of cigar smoke, everything seemed correct. Listening at his door he heard Mrs. Schiller exclaiming shrilly in the hall, assisted by yappings from the pug. Doors upstairs were opened, and questions were called out. He heard guttural groans from the bearded one, mingled with oaths and some angry remark about having fallen downstairs. The pug, frenzied with excitement, yelled insanely. A female voice—possibly Mrs. J. F. Smith—cried out "What's that smell of burning?" Someone else said, "They're burning feathers under his nose to bring him to."

"Yes, Hun's feathers," chuckled Aubrey to himself. He locked his door, and sat down by the window with his opera glasses.

===Chinese===
他一边布置一边忍不住窃笑，想起大学里也有过一次差不多的恶作剧，场景相似，目的可没这么严肃。他先脱掉鞋子，小心翼翼地放到一边，免得待会儿找起来耽误时间。然后，他选了离楼梯底部大约六英尺的一根栏杆，把铁丝一头牢牢拴在柱脚上，余下的部分在两级台阶上铺成一个大圈套。铁丝的另一头从栏杆间穿出去，拧成一个小环，方便拉动。接着他关掉了大厅的煤气灯，在黑暗中坐下来守株待兔。

他坐了很久，心里有些紧张，生怕那只哈巴狗溜达过来发现他。一个穿晨衣的女士——也许是J. F. 史密斯太太（Mrs. J. F. Smith）——从一楼房间走出来，从他身边极近的距离擦过，嘴里嘀咕着往楼上去了，着实吓了他一跳。他赶紧把绳套挪开，好在动作够快。不过不久，他的耐心得到了回报。他听到楼上一扇门吱呀一声，接着楼梯吱吱嘎嘎地呻吟起来，有人正在缓缓下楼。他重新布好陷阱，微笑着等待。某处钟声敲响十二点时，那人摸索着走完最后一段楼梯，在黑暗中探路。奥布里听见他低声咒骂。

就在那人的两只脚都踏进圈套的精确瞬间，奥布里猛地一拽铁丝。那人像保险柜一样一头栽下来，撞在栏杆上，四仰八叉地摔在地板上。这一跤摔得够狠，整栋房子都跟着震了。他躺在地上呻吟着，咒骂着。

奥布里好不容易忍住笑，划了一根火柴，举到那个四仰八叉的身影上方。那人侧着脸，紧贴着一只摊开的手臂，但大胡子一看便知。又是那位副厨师长，他似乎半昏迷了。"烧焦的毛发可是绝佳的兴奋剂，"奥布里自言自语，把火柴凑到那丛大胡子上。他心满意足地烧掉了两三英寸胡子，然后把康乃馨放在那个倒霉蛋的脑袋上。接着，他听到地下室有动静，赶紧收拾好铁丝和鞋子，逃上了楼。他回到自己房间时内心狂笑不止，但还是小心翼翼地推门进去，生怕有埋伏。除了弥漫着浓重的雪茄烟味之外，一切似乎都正常。他贴着门听，听见施勒太太在大厅里尖声大叫，哈巴狗在一旁汪汪助阵。楼上的门纷纷打开，人们喊着问出了什么事。他听到那个大胡子男人含混地呻吟着，夹杂着咒骂和几句关于摔下楼梯的愤怒抱怨。哈巴狗兴奋得发了疯，歇斯底里地狂吠。一个女声——也许是J. F. 史密斯太太——叫道："那股烧焦味是什么？"另一个人说："他们在用羽毛熏他的鼻子，想让他醒过来。"

"是啊，德国佬的毛，"奥布里暗自窃笑。他锁上门，拿着歌剧望远镜在窗边坐了下来。

## IX Again the Narrative Is Retarded / 九 叙事再次减速

===Original===
Roger had spent a quiet evening in the bookshop. Sitting at his desk under a fog of tobacco, he had honestly intended to do some writing on the twelfth chapter of his great work on bookselling. This chapter was to be an (alas, entirely conjectural) "Address Delivered by a Bookseller on Being Conferred the Honorary Degree of Doctor of Letters by a Leading University," and it presented so many alluring possibilities that Roger's mind always wandered from the paper into entranced visions of his imagined scene. He loved to build up in fancy the flattering details of that fine ceremony when bookselling would at last be properly recognized as one of the learned professions. He could see the great auditorium, filled with cultivated people: men with Emersonian profiles, ladies whispering behind their fluttering programmes. He could see the academic beadle, proctor, dean (or whatever he is, Roger was a little doubtful) pronouncing the august words of presentation—

===Chinese===
罗杰（Roger）在书店里度过了一个安静的夜晚。坐在烟雾缭绕的书桌前，他原本真心打算写点关于他那部关于卖书术的大作的第十二章。这一章将是一篇（唉，完全是臆想的）「一位书商在获颁一流大学荣誉文学博士学位时的致辞」，它展现了如此诱人的可能性，以至于罗杰的思绪总是从稿纸上飘散开去，沉入对想象中场景的迷醉遐想。他喜欢在幻想中精心构建那个美妙仪式的奉承细节——卖书术终于被恰当地承认是学者职业之一的时刻。他能看见大礼堂里坐满了有教养的人：有着爱默生式轮廓的男士们，以及在飘动的节目单后窃窃私语的女士们。他能看见学术执事、学监、院长（或者别的什么头衔，罗杰有点拿不准）庄严地宣读授予词——

===Original===
> A man who, in season and out of season, forgetting private gain for public weal, has laboured with Promethean and sacrificial ardour to instil the love of reasonable letters into countless thousands; to whom, and to whose colleagues, amid the perishable caducity of human affairs, is largely due the pullulation of literary taste; in honouring whom we seek to honour the noble and self-effacing profession of which he is so representative a member—

===Chinese===
> 此人不分寒暑，舍私利而谋公益，以普罗米修斯般的牺牲热情，致力于向无数人灌输对健全文学的热爱；在人类事务转瞬即逝的流变中，文学品味的滋长很大程度上归功于他及其同行；今日我们向他致敬，实为向这个高贵而忘我职业中如此典型的代表人物致敬——

===Original===
Then he could see the modest bookseller, somewhat clammy in his extremities and lost within his academic robe and hood, nervously fidgeting his mortarboard, haled forward by ushers, and tottering rubescent before the chancellor, provost, president (or whoever it might be) who hands out the diploma. Then (in Roger's vision) he could see the garlanded bibliopole turning to the expectant audience, giving his trailing gown a deft rearward kick as the ladies do on the stage, and uttering, without hesitation or embarrassment, with due interpolation of graceful pleasantry, that learned and unlaboured discourse on the delights of bookishness that he had often dreamed of. Then he could see the ensuing reception: the distinguished savants crowding round; the plates of macaroons, the cups of untasted tea; the ladies twittering, "Now there's something I want to ask you—why are there so many statues to generals, admirals, parsons, doctors, statesmen, scientists, artists, and authors, but no statues to booksellers?"

===Chinese===
然后他能看见那位谦逊的书商，四肢末端有点发冷，淹没在学术长袍和兜帽里，紧张地摆弄着学位帽，被引座员拽向前去，在颁发学位证书的校长、院长、教务长（或不管是谁）面前踉跄着，满脸通红。接着（在罗杰的想象中）他能看见头戴花冠的书商转向期待的观众，像舞台上的女士们一样灵巧地向后踢起拖地的长袍，毫不犹豫也毫不尴尬地，穿插着优雅的俏皮话，发表那篇他常常梦想的关于书卷气乐趣的博学而从容的演说。然后他能看见随后的招待会：杰出的学者们围拢过来；一盘盘杏仁饼，一杯杯未被品尝的茶；女士们叽叽喳喳地说：「现在我有个问题想问您——为什么有那么多将军、海军上将、牧师、医生、政治家、科学家、艺术家和作家的雕像，却没有书商的雕像？」

===Original===
Contemplation of this glittering scene always lured Roger into fantastic dreams. Ever since he had travelled country roads, some years before, selling books from a van drawn by a fat white horse, he had nourished a secret hope of some day founding a Parnassus on Wheels Corporation which would own a fleet of these vans and send them out into the rural byways where bookstores are unknown. He loved to imagine a great map of New York State, with the daily location of each travelling Parnassus marked by a coloured pin. He dreamed of himself, sitting in some vast central warehouse of secondhand books, poring over his map like a military chief of staff and forwarding cases of literary ammunition to various bases where his vans would restock. His idea was that his travelling salesmen could be recruited largely from college professors, parsons, and newspaper men, who were weary of their thankless tasks, and would welcome an opportunity to get out on the road. One of his hopes was that he might interest Mr. Chapman in this superb scheme, and he had a vision of the day when the shares of the Parnassus on Wheels Corporation would pay a handsome dividend and be much sought after by serious investors.

===Chinese===
对这辉煌场景的沉思总是将罗杰引入奇思妙想。自从几年前他乘着一辆由胖白马拉的大篷车在乡间道路上旅行卖书以来，他就暗自怀揣着一个希望：有朝一日成立一家「车轮上的帕纳索斯（Parnassus on Wheels）」公司，拥有一支这样的车队，将它们派往书店未及的乡间小路。他喜欢想象一张纽约州大地图，每个旅行的帕纳索斯用彩色图钉标记出每日位置。他梦想自己坐在某个巨大的二手书中央仓库里，像军队参谋长一样仔细研究地图，将一箱箱文学弹药转发到各个补给基地。他的想法是，他的旅行销售员可以从大学教授、牧师和记者中大量招募，这些人厌倦了他们吃力不讨好的工作，会欢迎有机会上路。他的希望之一是能让查普曼先生（Mr. Chapman）对这个绝妙的计划感兴趣，他想象着有朝一日「车轮上的帕纳索斯」公司的股票支付丰厚的股息，受到严肃投资者的追捧。

===Original===
These thoughts turned his mind toward his brother-in-law Andrew McGill, the author of several engaging books on the joys of country living, who dwells at the Sabine Farm in the green elbow of a Connecticut valley. The original Parnassus, a quaint old blue wagon in which Roger had lived and journeyed and sold books over several thousand miles of country roads in the days before his marriage, was now housed in Andrew's barn. Peg, his fat white horse, had lodging there also. It occurred to Roger that he owed Andrew a letter, and putting aside his notes for the bookseller's collegiate oration, he began to write:

===Chinese===
这些思绪将他的心绪引向了他的内兄安德鲁·麦吉尔（Andrew McGill），后者写过几本关于乡村生活乐趣的引人入胜的书，住在康涅狄格州一个绿色山谷弯处的萨宾农场（Sabine Farm）。那辆最初的帕纳索斯——一辆古雅的旧蓝色马车，罗杰婚前曾乘着它在数千英里的乡村道路上生活、旅行和卖书——如今安放在安德鲁的谷仓里。他的胖白马佩格（Peg）也寄宿在那里。罗杰想起他欠安德鲁一封信，于是放下书商学院致辞的笔记，开始写道：

===Original===
> It is scandalous not to have thanked you sooner for the annual cask of cider, which has given us even more than the customary pleasure. This has been an autumn when I have been hard put to it to keep up with my own thoughts, and I've written no letters at all. Like everyone else I am thinking constantly of this new peace that has marvellously come upon us. I trust we may have statesmen who will be able to turn it to the benefit of humanity. I wish there could be an international peace conference of booksellers, for (you will smile at this) my own conviction is that the future happiness of the world depends in no small measure on them and on the librarians. I wonder what a German bookseller is like?

===Chinese===
> 没有早日感谢你送来的每年一桶苹果酒，这真是失礼之至，它给我们带来了比往常更多的乐趣。今年秋天我一直在努力追赶自己的思绪，一封信也没写。和所有人一样，我时刻在思考这奇迹般降临的和平。我信任我们会有政治家能够将它转化为人类的福祉。我希望有一次国际书商和平会议，因为（你会对此发笑的）我个人相信，世界的未来幸福在很大程度上取决于他们和图书馆员们。不知道德国书商是什么样的人？

===Original===
> I've been reading *The Education of Henry Adams* and wish he might have lived long enough to give us his thoughts on the War. I fear it would have bowled him over. He thought that this is not a world "that sensitive and timid natures can regard without a shudder." What would he have said of the four-year shambles we have watched with sickened hearts?

===Chinese===
> 我一直在读《亨利·亚当斯的教育》（The Education of Henry Adams），真希望他能活得够久，让我们读到他对这场战争的看法。我怕那会让他震惊不已。他认为这不是一个「敏感而胆怯的心灵能不颤抖地看待」的世界。对于我们怀着病态心情目睹的四年血腥杀戮，他会怎么说呢？

===Original===
> You remember my favourite poem—old George Herbert's "Church Porch"—where he says—
> Well, I've been tumbling my thoughts up and down a good deal. Melancholy, I suppose, is the curse of the thinking classes; but I confess my soul wears a great uneasiness these days! The sudden and amazing turnover in human affairs, dramatic beyond anything in history, already seems to be taken as a matter of course. My great fear is that humanity will forget the atrocious sufferings of the war, which have never been told. I am hoping and praying that men like Philip Gibbs may tell us what they really saw.

===Chinese===
> 你记得我最喜爱的诗——老乔治·赫伯特（George Herbert）的《教堂门廊》（"Church Porch"）——他说——
> 唉，我一直在上下翻搅我的思绪。我想，忧郁是思想阶级的诅咒；但我承认我的灵魂这些日子承受着巨大的不安！人类事务中突然而惊人的剧变，比历史上任何事件都更富戏剧性，似乎已被视为理所当然。我最大的恐惧是人类会忘记战争的暴行，那些从未被讲述的苦难。我期待并祈祷像菲利普·吉布斯（Philip Gibbs）这样的人能告诉我们他们真正看到了什么。

===Original===
> You will not agree with me on what I am about to say, for I know you as a stubborn Republican; but I thank fortune that Wilson is going to the Peace Conference. I've been mulling over one of my favourite books—it lies beside me as I write—*Cromwell's Letters and Speeches*, edited by Carlyle, with what Carlyle amusingly calls "Elucidations." (Carlyle is not very good at "elucidating" anything!) I have heard somewhere or other that this is one of Wilson's favourite books, and indeed, there is much of the Cromwell in him. With what a grim, covenanting zeal he took up the sword when at last it was forced into his hand! And I have been thinking that what he will say to the Peace Conference will smack strongly of what old Oliver used to say to Parliament in 1657 and 1658—"If we will have Peace without a worm in it, lay we foundations of Justice and Righteousness." What makes Wilson so irritating to the unthoughtful is that he operates exclusively upon reason, not upon passion. He contradicts Kipling's famous lines, which apply to most men—

===Chinese===
> 你不会同意我接下来要说的话，因为我知道你是个顽固的共和党人；但我感谢命运，威尔逊（Wilson）将去参加和平会议。我一直在反复思考我最喜爱的一本书——它就在我写信时放在一旁——卡莱尔（Carlyle）编的《克伦威尔书信演讲集》（Cromwell's Letters and Speeches），带着卡莱尔戏称为「阐释」的东西。（卡莱尔不太擅长「阐释」任何事！）我不知在哪里听说这是威尔逊最喜爱的书之一，确实，他身上有许多克伦威尔的影子。当剑终于被迫塞进他手中时，他是带着多么冷峻的盟约热情握起它的！我一直在想，他在和平会议上说的话，会深深带有老奥利弗（Oliver）在1657和1658年对议会常说的味道——「如果我们想要没有蛀虫的和平，就要奠定正义与公义的基石。」让不假思索的人如此恼火的是，威尔逊完全诉诸理性，而非激情。他驳斥了吉卜林（Kipling）的名句，那名句适用于大多数人——

===Original===
> In this instance, I think, Reason is going to win. I feel the whole current of the world setting in that direction.

===Chinese===
> 在这个案例中，我认为理性将要胜利。我感到整个世界的潮流正朝着那个方向涌去。

===Original===
> It's quaint to think of old Woodrow, a kind of Cromwell-Wordsworth, going over to do his bit among the diplomatic shell-craters. What I'm waiting for is the day when he'll get back into private life and write a book about it. There's a job, if you like, for a man who might reasonably be supposed to be pretty tired in body and soul! When that book comes out I'll spend the rest of my life in selling it. I ask nothing better! Speaking of Wordsworth, I've often wondered whether Woodrow hasn't got some poems concealed somewhere among his papers! I've always imagined that he may have written poems on the sly. And by the way, you needn't make fun of me for being so devoted to George Herbert. Do you realize that two of the most familiar quotations in our language come from his pen, viz.:
> and
> Forgive this tedious sermon! My mind has been so tumbled up and down this autumn that I am in a queer state of mingled melancholy and exaltation. You know how much I live in and for books. Well, I have a curious feeling, a kind of premonition that there are great books coming out of this welter of human hopes and anguishes, perhaps A book in which the tempest-shaken soul of the race will speak out as it never has before. The Bible, you know, is rather a disappointment: it has never done for humanity what it should have done. I wonder why? Walt Whitman is going to do a great deal, but he is not quite what I mean. There is something coming—I don't know just what! I thank God I am a bookseller, trafficking in the dreams and beauties and curiosities of humanity rather than some mere huckster of merchandise. But how helpless we all are when we try to tell what goes on within us! I found this in one of Lafcadio Hearn's letters the other day—I marked the passage for you

===Chinese===
> 想想老伍德罗（Woodrow），一个克伦威尔-华兹华斯式的人物，跑到外交弹坑中去做他的一份，这真是妙不可言。我所等待的是他恢复平民身份并写一本书的那一天。对于一个可以合理地被认为在身心上都相当疲惫的人来说，这工作正合适！当那本书出版时，我将用余生来推销它。我别无他求！说到华兹华斯（Wordsworth），我常常想伍德罗是否在他文件某处藏着一些诗！我一直想象他可能偷偷写过诗。顺便说一句，你不必嘲笑我对乔治·赫伯特如此虔诚。你意识到吗，我们语言中最熟悉的两个引语出自他的笔下，即：
> 和
> 原谅这篇冗长的说教！今年秋天我的思绪如此上下翻搅，以至于我处于一种忧郁与亢奋交织的奇妙状态。你知道我多么为书而活、以书为生。嗯，我有一种奇怪的感觉，一种预感，伟大的书籍正从人类希望与苦难的混战中诞生，也许是一本种族在暴风雨中的灵魂前所未有的倾诉之书。你知道，《圣经》相当令人失望：它从未为人类做过它应该做的事。我纳闷为什么？沃尔特·惠特曼（Walt Whitman）将成就很多，但他并不完全是我所指的。某种东西正在来临——我不确定是什么！我感谢上帝我是个书商，经手的是人类的梦想、美和奇趣，而不是某种纯粹的商品小贩。但当我们试图倾诉内心所想时，我们是多么无助啊！前几天我在拉夫卡迪奥·赫恩（Lafcadio Hearn）的一封信中发现了这段话——我为你标出了这段文字

===Original===
> You can imagine what evenings I have here among my shelves, now the long dark nights are come! Of course until ten o'clock, when I shut up shop, I am constantly interrupted—as I have been during this letter, once to sell a copy of *Helen's Babies* and once to sell *The Ballad of Reading Gaol*, so you can see how varied are my clients' tastes! But later on, after we have had our evening cocoa and Helen has gone to bed, I prowl about the place, dipping into this and that, fuddling myself with speculation. How clear and bright the stream of the mind flows in those late hours, after all the sediment and floating trash of the day has drained off! Sometimes I seem to coast the very shore of Beauty or Truth, and hear the surf breaking on those shining sands. Then some offshore wind of weariness or prejudice bears me away again. Have you ever come across Andreyev's *Confessions of a Little Man During Great Days*? One of the honest books of the War. The Little Man ends his confession thus—

===Chinese===
> 你能想象我在这些书架间度过的夜晚吗，如今长夜已至！当然，直到十点我关店之前，我不断被打断——写这封信时也是如此，一次是卖掉一本《海伦的宝贝》（Helen's Babies），一次是卖掉一本《雷丁监狱之歌》（The Ballad of Reading Gaol），所以你看我的顾客口味多么多样！但后来，当我们喝完晚可可、海伦上床之后，我在店里四处游荡，这里翻翻那里看看，沉溺于遐想之中。在那些深夜时分，当白天的所有沉淀物和漂浮垃圾都已排干之后，思想之流流得多么清澈明亮啊！有时我仿佛滑行在美或真理的岸边，听见浪涛拍击那闪亮的沙滩。然后又一阵 offshore 的倦怠或偏见之风将我吹走。你有没有读过安德烈耶夫（Andreyev）的《小人物在伟大日子的忏悔》（Confessions of a Little Man During Great Days）？战争中诚实的书之一。小人物如此结束他的忏悔——

===Original===
> And of course, as soon as one puts one's self in that frame of mind someone comes along and picks your pocket… I suppose we must teach ourselves to be too proud to mind having our pockets picked!

===Chinese===
> 当然，一旦一个人处于那种心境，就有人过来掏你的口袋……我想我们必须教会自己骄傲到不在乎被掏口袋！

===Original===
> Did it ever occur to you that the world is really governed by *books*? The course of this country in the War, for instance, has been largely determined by the books Wilson has read since he first began to think! If we could have a list of the principal books he has read since the War began, how interesting it would be.

===Chinese===
> 你是否曾想过，世界实际上是由*书籍*统治的？例如，这个国家在战争中的进程，很大程度上是由威尔逊自从他开始思考以来所读的书籍决定的！如果我们能有一份他自开战以来所读主要书籍的清单，那该多有趣啊。

===Original===
> Here's something I'm just copying out to put up on my bulletin board for my customers to ponder. It was written by Charles Sorley, a young Englishman who was killed in France in 1915. He was only twenty years old—

===Chinese===
> 这里有我正抄写出来贴在我的告示牌上供顾客思考的东西。它是由查尔斯·索利（Charles Sorley）写的，一位1915年在法国阵亡的英国青年。他年仅二十岁——

===Original===
> Isn't that noble? You see what I am dumbly groping for—some way of thinking about the War that will make it seem (to future ages) a purification for humanity rather than a mere blackness of stinking cinders and tortured flesh and men shot to ribbons in marshes of blood and sewage. Out of such unspeakable desolation men *must* rise to some new conception of national neighbourhood. I hear so much apprehension that Germany won't be punished sufficiently for her crime. But how can any punishment be devised or imposed for such a huge panorama of sorrow? I think she has already punished herself horribly, and will continue to do so. My prayer is that what we have gone through will startle the world into some new realization of the sanctity of life—all life, animal as well as human. Don't you find that a visit to a zoo can humble and astound you with all that amazing and grotesque variety of living energy?

===Chinese===
> 这难道不崇高吗？你明白我在笨拙地摸索什么——某种思考战争的方式，让它在（后世看来）成为人类的净化，而不仅仅是恶臭灰烬、受折磨的肉体、在血与污水的沼泽中被射成碎片的人们的黑暗。从这种难以言喻的荒芜中，人类*必须*升起对民族邻里的新理解。我听到许多忧虑，担心德国不会因她的罪行受到足够惩罚。但对于如此巨大的悲伤全景，能设计或施加什么样的惩罚呢？我认为她已经可怕地惩罚了自己，并将继续如此。我祈祷我们所经历的一切能惊醒世界，让人类重新认识到生命的神圣——所有生命，无论是动物还是人类。你难道不觉得参观一次动物园会让你谦卑并震惊于那令人惊叹的、怪诞多样的生命能量吗？

===Original===
> What is it that we find in every form of life? Desire of some sort—some unexplained motive power that impels even the smallest insect on its queer travels. You must have watched some infinitesimal red spider on a fence rail, bustling along—why and whither? Who knows? And when you come to man, what a chaos of hungers and impulses keep thrusting him through his cycle of quaint tasks! And in every human heart you find some sorrow, some frustration, some lurking pang. I often think of Lafcadio Hearn's story of his Japanese cook. Hearn was talking of the Japanese habit of not showing their emotions on their faces. His cook was a smiling, healthy, agreeable-looking young fellow whose face was always cheerful. Then one day, by chance, Hearn happened to look through a hole in the wall and saw his cook alone. His face was not the same face. It was thin and drawn and showed strange lines worn by old hardships or sufferings. Hearn thought to himself, "He will look just like that when he is dead." He went into the kitchen to see him, and instantly the cook was all changed, young and happy again. Never again did Hearn see that face of trouble; but he knew the man wore it when he was alone.

===Chinese===
> 我们在生命的各种形式中发现的是什么？是某种欲望——某种未解释的驱动力，甚至驱使最小的昆虫踏上它奇妙的旅程。你一定观察过栏杆上一只微小的红蜘蛛，匆忙爬行——为什么？去哪里？谁知道呢？当你观察人类时，多少饥饿与冲动的混沌不断推着他度过他古怪任务的循环！而在每个人心中，你都能发现某种悲伤、某种挫败、某种潜伏的痛苦。我常常想起拉夫卡迪奥·赫恩（Lafcadio Hearn）关于他日本厨师的故事。赫恩正在谈论日本人不在脸上表露情感的习惯。他的厨师是个笑容满面、健康、和善的年轻人，脸上总是洋溢着欢乐。然后有一天，偶然间，赫恩透过墙上的一个洞看到他的厨师独自一人。他的脸不再是同一张脸了。它瘦削而紧绷，显露出由旧日艰辛或苦难刻下的奇怪纹路。赫恩心想：「他死后就会是那副模样。」他走进厨房去看他，厨师立刻变了，又年轻快乐起来。赫恩再也没有见过那张忧愁的脸；但他知道那人在独处时戴着它。

===Original===
> Don't you think there is a kind of parable there for the race as a whole? Have you ever met a man without wondering what shining sorrows he hides from the world, what contrast between vision and accomplishment torments him? Behind every smiling mask is there not some cryptic grimace of pain? Henry Adams puts it tersely. He says the human mind appears suddenly and inexplicably out of some unknown and unimaginable void. It passes half its known life in the mental chaos of sleep. Even when awake it is a victim of its own ill-adjustment, of disease, of age, of external suggestion, of nature's compulsions; it doubts its own sensations and trusts only in instruments and averages. After sixty years or so of growing astonishment the mind wakes to find itself looking blankly into the void of death. And, as Adams says, that it should profess itself pleased by this performance is all that the highest rules of good breeding can ask. That the mind should actually be satisfied would prove that it exists only as idiocy!

===Chinese===
> 难道你不觉得这为整个人类提供了一种寓言吗？你遇见一个人时，难道不曾疑惑他向世界隐藏了什么闪光的忧伤，什么理想与成就之间的反差在折磨他？在每一张微笑的面具背后，难道没有某种神秘的痛苦鬼脸吗？亨利·亚当斯（Henry Adams）简洁地表达了它。他说人类心智突然而莫名其妙地从某个未知且不可想象的虚空中出现。它将已知生命的一半消磨在睡眠的心智混沌中。即使清醒时，它也是自身失调、疾病、衰老、外部暗示、自然强制的受害者；它怀疑自己的感觉，只信任仪器和平均值。经过大约六十年不断增长的惊讶后，心智醒来发现自己茫然凝视着死亡的虚空。正如亚当斯所说，它声称对此感到满意，这已是最高教养规则所能要求的全部。心智若真的心满意足，那将证明它只以白痴的形式存在！

===Original===
> I hope that you will write to tell me along what curves your mind is moving. For my own part I feel that we are on the verge of amazing things. Long ago I fell back on books as the only permanent consolers. They are the one stainless and unimpeachable achievement of the human race. It saddens me to think that I shall have to die with thousands of books unread that would have given me noble and unblemished happiness. I will tell you a secret. I have never read *King Lear*, and have purposely refrained from doing so. If I were ever very ill I would only need to say to myself "You can't die yet, you haven't read *Lear*." That would bring me round, I know it would.

===Chinese===
> 我希望你能写信告诉我你的思想正沿着哪些曲线行进。就我而言，我觉得我们正处在惊人事物的边缘。很久以前我就退守到书籍身边，视其为唯一持久的慰藉。它们是人类唯一无瑕且无可指摘的成就。想到我将带着数千本未读的、本可以给我带来高尚而纯洁快乐的书死去，我就感到悲伤。我要告诉你一个秘密。我从未读过《李尔王》（King Lear），并且故意没有去读。如果有一天我病得很重，我只需要对自己说「你还不能死，你还没读《李尔王》呢。」那会让我恢复过来的，我知道会的。

===Original===
> You see, books are the answer to all our perplexities! Henry Adams grinds his teeth at his inability to understand the universe. The best he can do is to suggest a "law of acceleration," which seems to mean that nature is hustling man along at an ever-increasing rate so that he will either solve all her problems or else die of fever in the effort. But Adams' candid portrait of a mind grappling helplessly with its riddles is so triumphantly delightful that one forgets the futility of the struggle in the accuracy of the picture. Man is unconquerable because he can make even his helplessness so entertaining. His motto seems to be "Even though He slay me, yet will I make fun of Him!"

===Chinese===
> 你看，书籍是我们所有困惑的答案！亨利·亚当斯对他无法理解宇宙这件事咬牙切齿。他所能做的最好的事情是提出一个「加速定律」，这似乎意味着自然正以越来越快的速度催促人类前进，以便他要么解决她所有的问题，要么在努力中死于热病。但亚当斯对一颗心智无助地应对谜题的坦率描绘是如此胜利般地令人愉悦，以至于人们忘记了挣扎的徒劳，只专注于描绘的精确。人类是不可征服的，因为他甚至能让自己的无助变得如此有趣。他的座右铭似乎是「纵然他杀我，我仍要嘲笑他！」

===Original===
> Yes, books are man's supreme triumph, for they gather up and transmit all other triumphs. As Walter de la Mare writes, "How uncomprehendingly must an angel from heaven smile on a poor human sitting engrossed in a romance: angled upon his hams, motionless in his chair, spectacles on nose, his two feet as close together as the flukes of a merman's tail, only his strange eyes stirring in his timeworn face."

===Chinese===
> 是的，书籍是人类至高的胜利，因为它们收集并传递所有其他胜利。正如沃尔特·德拉·梅尔（Walter de la Mare）所写：「一位天堂的天使会多么不解地俯视一个沉浸在小说中的可怜人类：他盘腿而坐，在椅子上一动不动，鼻梁上架着眼镜，两只脚紧靠在一起如同人鱼尾巴的分叉，只有他奇怪的眼睛在他苍老的脸上转动。」

===Original===
> Well, I've been scribbling away all this time and haven't given you any news whatever. Helen came back the other day from a visit to Boston where she enjoyed herself greatly. Tonight she has gone out to the movies with a young protégée of ours, Miss Titania Chapman, an engaging damsel whom we have taken in as an apprentice bookseller. It's a quaint idea, done at the request of her father, Mr. Chapman, the proprietor of Chapman's Daintybits which you see advertised everywhere. He is a great booklover, and is very eager to have the zeal transmitted to his daughter. So you can imagine my glee to have a neophyte of my own to preach books at! Also it will enable me to get away from the shop a little more. I had a telephone call from Philadelphia this afternoon asking me to go over there on Monday evening to make an estimate of the value of a private collection that is to be sold. I was rather flattered because I can't imagine how they got hold of my name.

===Chinese===
> 好了，我写了这么久，却什么消息也没告诉你。海伦前几天从波士顿回来，玩得很开心。今晚她和我们年轻的被保护人蒂塔尼亚·查普曼（Titania Chapman）小姐一起去看电影了，这是个迷人的姑娘，我们收她做学徒书商。这是个古怪的想法，是应她父亲查普曼先生的要求做的，他就是你在到处广告中看到的查普曼「美味一口」（Chapman's Daintybits）的老板。他是个大书虫，非常渴望将这种热情传递给他的女儿。所以你可以想象我有多高兴，终于有个新手让我向他布道书本了！而且这也能让我稍微多离开书店一点。今天下午我接到费城的电话，让我周一晚上去那里估价一个即将出售的私人藏书。我相当受宠若惊，因为我不知道他们是怎么弄到我的名字的。

===Original===
> Forgive this long, incoherent scrawl. How did you like *Erewhon*? It's pretty near closing time and I must say grace over the day's accounts.

===Chinese===
> 原谅这封冗长、语无伦次的涂鸦。你觉得《埃瑞璜》（Erewhon）怎么样？快到关门时间了，我得为今天的账目做祷告了。

===Original===
> Yours ever,
> Roger Mifflin.

===Chinese===
> 永远属于你的，
> 罗杰·米夫林（Roger Mifflin）。

## X Roger Raids the Icebox / X 罗杰夜袭冰箱

===Original===
Roger had just put Carlyle’s *Cromwell* back in its proper place in the History alcove when Helen and Titania returned from the movies. Bock, who had been dozing under his master’s chair, rose politely and wagged a deferential tail.
"I do think Bock has the darlingest manners," said Titania.
"Yes," said Helen, "it’s really a marvel that his wagging muscles aren’t all worn out, he has abused them so."
"Well," said Roger, "did you have a good time?"

===Chinese===
罗杰（Roger）刚把卡莱尔（Carlyle）编的《克伦威尔书信演讲集》放回历史类书架的合适位置，海伦（Helen）和蒂塔尼亚（Titania）就从电影院回来了。博克（Bock）——一直打盹趴在主人椅子底下的那条芥末色梗犬——礼貌地站起来，摇了摇恭顺的尾巴。
"我觉得博克的教养真是好极了，"蒂塔尼亚说。
"可不是嘛，"海伦说，"摇尾巴的肌肉居然还没给磨坏，真是个奇迹——他摇得也太厉害了。"
"怎么样，"罗杰说，"玩得开心吗？"

===Original===
"An adorable time!" cried Titania, with a face and voice so sparkling that two musty habitués of the shop popped their heads out of the alcoves marked "Essays" and "Theology" and peered in amazement. One of these even went so far as to purchase the copy of Leigh Hunt’s *Wishing Cap Papers* he had been munching through, in order to have an excuse to approach the group and satisfy his bewildered eyes. When Miss Chapman took the book and wrapped it for him, his astonishment was made complete.
Unconscious that she was actually creating business, Titania resumed.
"We met your friend Mr. Gilbert on the street," she said, "and he went to the movies with us. He says he’s coming in on Monday to fix the furnace while you’re away."

===Chinese===
"开心极了！"蒂塔尼亚嚷道，脸上的光彩和嗓音里的欢快是如此耀眼，以至于店里两位老书虫——分别从标着"随笔"和"神学"的凹室里探出头来，惊讶地张望。其中一位甚至掏钱买下了他一直翻啃的那本利·亨特（Leigh Hunt）的《许愿帽文集》，就为了找个借口走近那群人，好好打量个够。当查普曼小姐接过书替他包好的时候，他简直目瞪口呆了。
蒂塔尼亚浑然不知自己正在招揽生意，继续说道：
"我们在街上遇见了你的朋友吉尔伯特先生（Mr. Gilbert），"她说，"他跟我们一起去看电影了。他说周一你出门的时候，他来修炉子。"

===Original===
"Well," said Roger, "these advertising agencies are certainly enterprising, aren’t they? Think of sending a man over to attend to my furnace, just on the slim chance of getting my advertising account."
"Did you have a quiet evening?" said Helen.
"I spent most of the time writing to Andrew," said Roger. "One amusing thing happened, though. I actually sold that copy of *Philip Dru*."
"No!" cried Helen.

===Chinese===
"哎呀，"罗杰说，"这些广告公司可真有魄力，不是吗？居然派人来帮我修炉子——就为那一点点揽到我广告生意的机会。"
"你今晚过得安静吗？"海伦问。
"大半时间都在给安德鲁（Andrew）写信，"罗杰说，"不过有件趣事——我真把那本《菲利普·德鲁》给卖出去了。"
"不会吧！"海伦叫道。

===Original===
"A fact," said Roger. "A man was looking at it, and I told him it was supposed to be written by Colonel House. He insisted on buying it. But what a sell when he tries to read it!"
"Did Colonel House really write it?" asked Titania.
"I don’t know," said Roger. "I hope not, because I find in myself a secret tendency to believe that Mr. House is an able man. If he did write it, I devoutly hope none of the foreign statesmen in Paris will learn of that fact."

===Chinese===
"千真万确，"罗杰说，"有个男人在翻那本书，我告诉他据说是豪斯上校（Colonel House）写的。他非要买不可。等他读起来的时候，那才叫上当呢！"
"豪斯上校真的是作者吗？"蒂塔尼亚问。
"不知道，"罗杰说，"我希望不是。因为我发现自己隐隐觉得豪斯先生是个有本事的人。假如真是他写的，我虔诚地祈祷巴黎的外国政要们千万别知道这事。"

===Original===
While Helen and Titania took off their wraps, Roger was busy closing up the shop. He went down to the corner with Bock to mail his letter, and when he returned to the den Helen had prepared a large jug of cocoa. They sat down by the fire to enjoy it.
"Chesterton has written a very savage poem against cocoa," said Roger, "which you will find in *The Flying Inn*; but for my part I find it the ideal evening drink. It lets the mind down gently, and paves the way for slumber. I have often noticed that the most terrific philosophical agonies can be allayed by three cups of Mrs. Mifflin’s cocoa. A man can safely read Schopenhauer all evening if he has a tablespoonful of cocoa and a tin of condensed milk available. Of course it should be made with condensed milk, which is the only way."

===Chinese===
海伦和蒂塔尼亚脱外套的工夫，罗杰忙着关店。他带着博克走到街角去寄信，回来时海伦已经在书房里备好了一大壶可可。三人围坐在炉火旁享用起来。
"切斯特顿（Chesterton）写过一首挖苦可可的毒辣诗，"罗杰说，"收录在《飞酒馆》里。不过依我看，可可是理想的晚间饮品——它让心绪缓缓沉淀，为睡眠铺好路。我常注意到，三杯米夫林太太（Mrs. Mifflin）煮的可可，足以平息任何可怕的哲学煎熬。只要手边有一大匙可可和一罐炼乳，一个人整个晚上读叔本华（Schopenhauer）都安然无恙。当然必须用炼乳来做，这是唯一正确的方式。"

===Original===
"I had no idea anything could be so good," said Titania. "Of course, Daddy makes condensed milk in one of his factories, but I never dreamed of trying it. I thought it was only used by explorers, people at the North Pole, you know."
"How stupid of me!" exclaimed Roger. "I quite forgot to tell you! Your father called up just after you had gone out this evening, and wanted to know how you were getting on."
"Oh, dear," said Titania. "He must have been delighted to hear I was at the movies, on the second day of my first job! He probably said it was just like me."

===Chinese===
"我从不知道有什么东西能这么好喝，"蒂塔尼亚说，"当然，老爸的工厂就产炼乳，可我从没想过要试。我以为只有探险家才用，你知道的，北极那些人。"
"我真糊涂！"罗杰一拍脑袋，"我居然忘了告诉你——你刚出门你父亲就打来电话，问你过得怎么样。"
"噢，天哪，"蒂塔尼亚说，"他听说我上班第二天就去看电影，一定高兴得很吧！他八成又说'果然是她的作风'。"

===Original===
"I explained that I had insisted on your going with Mrs. Mifflin, because I felt she needed the change."
"I do hope," said Titania, "you won’t let Daddy poison your mind about me. He thinks I’m dreadfully frivolous, just because I *look* frivolous. But I’m so keen to make good in this job. I’ve been practicing doing up parcels all afternoon, so as to learn how to tie the string nicely and not cut it until after the knot’s tied. I found that when you cut it beforehand either you get it too short and it won’t go round, or else too long and you waste some. Also I’ve learned how to make wrapping paper cuffs to keep my sleeves clean."

===Chinese===
"我解释说，是我坚持让你跟米夫林太太一起去的，因为我觉得她也需要换换心情。"
"我真的希望，"蒂塔尼亚说，"你别让老爸把你脑子里对我印象搞坏了。他觉得我轻浮得不得了——就因为我*看起来*轻浮。可我真的很想把这份工作干好。整个下午我都在练习包书，学怎么把绳子系漂亮，学到结打好之前绝不先剪断。我发现要是提前剪好，不是太短绑不住，就是太长浪费了。我还学会了用包装纸做袖套，免得弄脏袖子。"

===Original===
"Well, I haven’t finished yet," continued Roger. "Your father wants us all to spend tomorrow out at your home. He wants to show us some books he has just bought, and besides he thinks maybe you’re feeling homesick."
"What, with all these lovely books to read? Nonsense! I don’t want to go home for six months!"
"He wouldn’t take No for an answer. He’s going to send Edwards round with the car the first thing tomorrow morning."
"What fun!" said Helen. "It’ll be delightful."

===Chinese===
"哎，我还没说完呢，"罗杰继续道，"你父亲想让我们明天都去你家待一天。他要给我们看几本新买的书，另外他觉得你可能想家了。"
"什么？有这么多可爱的书等着我看，谁还会想家？胡说！我半年都不想回去！"
"他可不接受拒绝。他要让爱德华兹（Edwards）明儿一大早就开车来接。"
"那多好啊！"海伦说，"一定很愉快。"

===Original===
"Goodness," said Titania. "Imagine leaving this adorable bookshop to spend Sunday in Larchmont. Well, I’ll be able to get that georgette blouse I forgot."
"What time will the car be here?" asked Helen.
"Mr. Chapman said about nine o’clock. He begs us to get out there as early as possible, as he wants to spend the day showing us his books."
As they sat round the fading bed of coals, Roger began hunting along his private shelves. "Have you ever read any Gissing?" he said.

===Chinese===
"天哪，"蒂塔尼亚说，"想想吧，要离开这可爱的书店，去拉奇蒙特（Larchmont）过周日。好吧，至少我能去拿那件忘拿的乔其纱衬衫。"
"车几点到？"海伦问。
"查普曼先生说大概九点。他求我们尽量早点过去，他想花一整天给我们看他的书。"
三人围坐在渐暗的余烬旁，罗杰开始在自己的私人书架上翻找。"你读过吉辛（Gissing）吗？"他问。

===Original===
Titania made a pathetic gesture to Mrs. Mifflin. "It’s awfully embarrassing to be asked these things! No, I never heard of him."
"Well, as the street we live on is named after him, I think you ought to," he said. He pulled down his copy of *The House of Cobwebs*. "I’m going to read you one of the most delightful short stories I know. It’s called 'A Charming Family.' "
"No, Roger," said Mrs. Mifflin firmly. "Not tonight. It’s eleven o’clock, and I can see Titania’s tired. Even Bock has left us and gone in to his kennel. He’s got more sense than you have."

===Chinese===
蒂塔尼亚朝米夫林太太做了个可怜巴巴的手势。"人家问这种问题真是让人难为情！不，我从没听说过他。"
"可是我们住的这条街就是以他的名字命名的，我觉得你应该读一读，"罗杰说着，从架子上抽出一册《蛛网之屋》。"我来给你读一篇我所知道的最精彩的短篇小说——叫《温馨一家人》。"
"不行，罗杰，"米夫林太太断然说道，"今晚不行。都十一点了，蒂塔尼亚明显困了。连博克都丢下我们回狗窝了——他可比你有脑子。"

===Original===
"All right," said the bookseller amiably. "Miss Chapman, you take the book up with you and read it in bed if you want to. Are you a librocubicularist?"
Titania looked a little scandalized.
"It’s all right, my dear," said Helen. "He only means are you fond of reading in bed. I’ve been waiting to hear him work that word into the conversation. He made it up, and he’s immensely proud of it."

===Chinese===
"好吧，"书商温和地说，"查普曼小姐，你把这本书带上楼去，想在床上读就读吧。你是librocubicularist（卧读爱好者）吗？"
蒂塔尼亚看起来有点惊骇。
"没关系的，亲爱的，"海伦说，"他不过是问你喜不喜欢躺在床上看书。我一直在等他把这个词塞进对话里——这是他自己编的，他得意得很呢。"

===Original===
"Reading in bed?" said Titania. "What a quaint idea! Does anyone do it? It never occurred to me. I’m sure when I go to bed I’m far too sleepy to think of such a thing."
"Run along then, both of you," said Roger. "Get your beauty sleep. I shan’t be very late."
He meant it when he said it, but returning to his desk at the back of the shop his eye fell upon his private shelf of books which he kept there "to rectify perturbations" as Burton puts it.

===Chinese===
"在床上看书？"蒂塔尼亚说，"多古怪的念头！有人这么做吗？我从来没想过。我敢说上了床，困都困死了，哪还想得到这个。"
"那你们俩快去睡吧，"罗杰说，"睡个美容觉。我不会太晚的。"
他说这话时是认真的。可是回到书店后面自己桌前，目光落在了那排私人藏书上——用伯顿（Burton）的话说，那是他用来"矫正心绪"的。

===Original===
On this shelf there stood *Pilgrim’s Progress*, *Shakespeare*, *The Anatomy of Melancholy*, *The Home Book of Verse*, *George Herbert’s Poems*, *The Notebooks of Samuel Butler*, and *Leaves of Grass*. He took down *The Anatomy of Melancholy*, that most delightful of all books for midnight browsing. Turning to one of his favourite passages—"A Consolatory Digression, Containing the Remedies of All Manner of Discontents"—he was happily lost to all ticking of the clock, retaining only such bodily consciousness as was needful to dump, fill, and relight his pipe from time to time. Solitude is a dear jewel for men whose days are spent in the tedious this-and-that of trade. Roger was a glutton for his midnight musings. To such tried companions as Robert Burton and George Herbert he was wont to exonerate his spirit. It used to amuse him to think of Burton, the lonely Oxford scholar, writing that vast book to "rectify" his own melancholy.
By and by, turning over the musty old pages, he came to the following, on Sleep—

===Chinese===
架上立着《天路历程》、《莎士比亚》、《忧郁的解剖》、《家庭诗选》、《乔治·赫伯特诗集》、《塞缪尔·巴特勒笔记》和《草叶集》。他取下《忧郁的解剖》——所有午夜闲翻书中最令人愉悦的一本。翻到他最爱的篇章之一——"一段安慰性离题：论一切不满之疗法"——他愉快地沉入忘我之境，时钟的嘀嗒声全然消散，只保留够倒烟灰、装烟丝、重新点着烟斗的最低限度的躯体知觉。对于终日困于琐碎营生的人来说，独处是一颗珍宝。罗杰对深夜沉思贪得无厌。他习惯向罗伯特·伯顿（Robert Burton）和乔治·赫伯特（George Herbert）这样久经考验的伙伴倾吐心事。想到伯顿——那个孤独的牛津学者——写下那部浩繁巨著来"矫正"自己的忧郁，他常觉得有趣。
翻着翻着泛黄的旧页，他读到了下面这段关于睡眠的文字——

===Original===
>The fittest time is two or three hours after supper, whenas the meat is now settled at the bottom of the stomach, and ’tis good to lie on the right side first, because at that site the liver doth rest under the stomach, not molesting any way, but heating him as a fire doth a kettle, that is put to it. After the first sleep ’tis not amiss to lie on the left side, that the meat may the better descend, and sometimes again on the belly, but never on the back. Seven or eight hours is a competent time for a melancholy man to rest—

===Chinese===
>最宜的时辰是饭后两三个钟头，此时食物已沉落胃底，宜先右侧卧，因该姿势下肝脏安歇于胃之下，不作搅扰，反以炉火温壶之势暖之。初醒之后，不妨改为左侧卧，使食物更顺畅地下行，有时亦可俯卧，唯独不可仰卧。忧郁之人睡七到八个时辰足矣——

===Original===
In that case, thought Roger, it’s time for me to be turning in. He looked at his watch, and found it was half-past twelve. He switched off his light and went back to the kitchen quarters to tend the furnace.
I hesitate to touch upon a topic of domestic bitterness, but candor compels me to say that Roger’s evening vigils invariably ended at the icebox. There are two theories as to this subject of icebox plundering, one of the husband and the other of the wife.

===Chinese===
如此说来，罗杰想，我该上床了。他看了看表——十二点半了。他关掉灯，回到厨房那边去照看炉子。
我不太想触碰一个家庭苦涩的话题，但坦诚迫使我如实交代：罗杰的夜读总以光顾冰箱告终。关于"洗劫冰箱"这件事，丈夫和妻子各执一词。

===Original===
Husbands are prone to think (in their simplicity) that if they take a little of everything palatable they find in the refrigerator, but thus distributing their forage over the viands the general effect of the depradation will be almost unnoticeable. Whereas wives say (and Mrs. Mifflin had often explained to Roger) that it is far better to take all of any one dish than a little of each; for the latter course is likely to diminish each item below the bulk at which it is still useful as a leftover. Roger, however, had the obstinate viciousness of all good husbands, and he knew the delights of cold provender by heart.
